## 73. Genuine Stress Incontinence

**PEARL:** **TYPE III genuine stress incontinence is due to INTRINSIC SPHINCTER DEFICIENCY.** **TYPE I — loss of the posterior urethrovesical angle. TYPE II — loss of that angle PLUS URETHRAL HYPERMOBILITY. TYPE III — a fixed, non-functioning urethra ("lead-pipe" or "drainpipe" urethra).** GSI is **involuntary leakage on raised intra-abdominal pressure WITHOUT detrusor contraction.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The distinction matters because it **determines the operation**. Types I and II are **anatomical** — the urethra is intrinsically competent but descends out of the abdominal pressure zone, so **re-suspending or supporting it (a MID-URETHRAL SLING, TVT or TOT, or a Burch colposuspension) restores continence.** Type III is a **functional failure of the sphincter itself**, which no amount of support will fix — hence **bulking agents, a pubovaginal fascial sling or an artificial urinary sphincter.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Severity grading: Grade 1 — leak on SEVERE stress (coughing, sneezing, jumping); Grade 2 — on MODERATE stress (walking fast, climbing stairs); Grade 3 — on MILD stress (standing up, or even at rest).**
- **Distinguish the incontinence types: STRESS — leak on effort, no urge, small volumes. URGE (overactive bladder/detrusor overactivity) — sudden urgency, nocturia, large-volume leaks; treat with bladder training, ANTIMUSCARINICS (oxybutynin, solifenacin — beware dry mouth, constipation and confusion in the elderly), MIRABEGRON, and botulinum toxin. MIXED — both. OVERFLOW — chronic retention with dribbling. CONTINUOUS leak with normal voiding → FISTULA or an ECTOPIC URETER.**
- **Investigations: a bladder diary, cough stress test, post-void residual volume, urinalysis to exclude infection, and URODYNAMICS before surgery or when the picture is unclear (to confirm that leakage is not detrusor-driven).**
- **FIRST-LINE TREATMENT FOR ALL TYPES OF STRESS INCONTINENCE IS SUPERVISED PELVIC FLOOR MUSCLE TRAINING for at least 3 months**, with weight loss, smoking cessation, treatment of constipation and cough, and vaginal oestrogen in postmenopausal women. **DULOXETINE is the only useful drug.**
- **Risk factors: vaginal delivery (especially instrumental and prolonged second stage), pudendal nerve injury, ageing and menopause, obesity, chronic cough and constipation, and previous pelvic surgery.**
- **The Q-TIP TEST demonstrates urethral hypermobility (>30° excursion on straining).**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Type III GSI is due to" → **intrinsic sphincter deficiency**.

**REMEMBER:** *Types I and II are a urethra that has fallen; type III is a urethra that has failed — and supporting it will not help.*

`Q11114`

---

## 74. Sperm–Mucus Interaction Tests

**PEARL:** In the **MILLER–KURZROK TEST** (an in-vitro sperm–mucus interaction test), cervical mucus is placed alongside the husband's semen on a slide **AT THE TIME OF OVULATION**, and **penetration of >3 cm in 30 minutes is NORMAL.** **JERKY, SHAKING motility after penetration is ABNORMAL** — it indicates **ANTISPERM ANTIBODIES.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Normal periovulatory mucus is **thin, watery, alkaline and arranged in parallel micelles** under oestrogen, forming channels that guide sperm upward — hence rapid, straight-line progression. When **antisperm antibodies (in either partner) coat the sperm, they agglutinate against the mucus micelles**, producing the characteristic **"shaking phenomenon"** — sperm that vibrate on the spot instead of advancing. The sign therefore points to an immunological, not a mechanical, cause of infertility.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The related tests: POSTCOITAL (SIMS–HUHNER) TEST — an IN-VIVO test performed 2–8 hours after intercourse at midcycle, examining cervical mucus for motile sperm (normal ≥10–20 motile sperm per high-power field). SEMEN–CERVICAL MUCUS CONTACT test and the SPERM PENETRATION (hamster egg) assay are the others.** **All of these are now largely OBSOLETE — they have poor predictive value and have been superseded by semen analysis and by IUI/IVF.**
- **Cervical mucus at ovulation shows FERNING (arborisation on drying, due to sodium chloride crystallisation) and SPINNBARKEIT (stretchability >8–10 cm). Progesterone in the luteal phase abolishes both — the mucus becomes thick, scanty and cellular.**
- **Basic infertility evaluation: SEMEN ANALYSIS FIRST; documentation of OVULATION (mid-luteal progesterone >5 ng/mL, or a biphasic basal body temperature chart); and TUBAL PATENCY (HSG, then laparoscopy with chromopertubation).**
- **WHO 2021 semen reference values: volume ≥1.4 mL, concentration ≥16 million/mL, total motility ≥42%, progressive motility ≥30%, normal morphology ≥4%. Abstinence 2–7 days, and abnormal results must be REPEATED after 3 months (a full spermatogenic cycle is ~74 days).**
- **Terminology: OLIGOZOOSPERMIA (low count), ASTHENOZOOSPERMIA (poor motility), TERATOZOOSPERMIA (abnormal forms), AZOOSPERMIA (none — obstructive versus non-obstructive, distinguished by FSH and testicular volume).**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "False about the Miller–Kurzrok test" → **jerky motility is normal** (it is abnormal, and suggests antisperm antibodies).

**REMEMBER:** *Three centimetres in thirty minutes; sperm that shake instead of swim are antibody-coated.*

`Q11115`

---

## 75. Types of Hysterectomy for Cervical Cancer

**PEARL:** **PIVER–RUTLEDGE TYPE II (MODIFIED RADICAL/Wertheim) hysterectomy removes the MEDIAL HALF of the cardinal (Mackenrodt's) and uterosacral ligaments, the UPPER 1–2 cm OF VAGINA, and clinically enlarged nodes** — with the ureter unroofed but preserved laterally. **TYPE III (RADICAL/Meigs) removes the ENTIRE cardinal and uterosacral ligaments at the pelvic side wall plus the UPPER 3–4 cm of vagina with pelvic lymphadenectomy.** **Type I is a simple extrafascial hysterectomy; Types IV and V are extended/ultra-radical.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The types form a **graded scale of PARAMETRIAL resection**, because **cervical cancer spreads laterally through the parametrium along the cardinal ligaments**. The more the parametrium is removed, the greater the clearance — and the greater the morbidity, since **the ureter, bladder and pelvic autonomic nerves all traverse that tissue.** That is why the type is matched to the stage rather than always taking the most radical option.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Radical surgery is offered for stage IA2–IB2/IIA1 disease. From STAGE IIB onwards, CONCURRENT CHEMORADIATION (cisplatin) is standard — surgery is not.** **Stage IA1 (≤3 mm invasion, no lymphovascular invasion) can be treated by conisation alone if fertility is desired, or simple hysterectomy.**
- **RADICAL TRACHELECTOMY (removal of the cervix, upper vagina and parametrium with a cerclage, preserving the uterine body) is the fertility-sparing option for small early tumours.**
- **The major morbidities of radical hysterectomy are BLADDER DYSFUNCTION (from autonomic nerve injury — hence NERVE-SPARING techniques), ureteric fistula and stricture, lymphocyst and LYMPHOEDEMA, and sexual dysfunction from vaginal shortening.**
- **The OVARIES are usually PRESERVED in young women with squamous cancer (ovarian metastasis is rare) and may be TRANSPOSED out of the radiation field.**
- **VULVAL cancer: radiotherapy is indicated for STAGE III and IV disease and for stage I–II with POSITIVE NODES, positive or close margins, or lymphovascular invasion — node-negative early disease is treated surgically alone.**
- **Cervical cancer remains a leading cancer in Indian women; the answer to it is HPV VACCINATION plus SCREENING, not better surgery.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Not a feature of modified radical hysterectomy" → **removal of the upper 4 cm of vagina** (that is Type III).

**REMEMBER:** *Type II takes half the parametrium and 2 cm of vagina; type III takes all of it and 3–4 cm.*

`Q11116, Q11117`

---

## 76. White's Classification of Diabetes in Pregnancy

**PEARL:** **CLASS A1 (diet-controlled gestational diabetes) is the ONLY class managed by DIET ALONE; A2 and ALL the pre-gestational classes (B, C, D, F, R, H, T) require INSULIN.** **B — onset after age 20 with duration <10 years. C — onset at 10–19 years or duration 10–19 years. D — onset before 10 years, duration >20 years, or BENIGN RETINOPATHY. F — NEPHROPATHY. R — PROLIFERATIVE RETINOPATHY. H — ISCHAEMIC HEART DISEASE. T — prior renal TRANSPLANT.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The classification's real message is that **DURATION AND END-ORGAN DAMAGE, not the blood glucose of the day, determine outcome.** A woman with class F or R disease has **already-diseased vasculature**, so she is at high risk of superimposed pre-eclampsia, growth restriction and perinatal loss — because the placenta depends on the same microvasculature. This is why the classification, though no longer used formally, still teaches the correct prognostic instinct.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **CLASS H (ischaemic heart disease) carries the HIGHEST MATERNAL MORTALITY of any class — pregnancy may be contraindicated.** **CLASS F (nephropathy) predicts pre-eclampsia, growth restriction and preterm delivery.**
- **DIABETIC RETINOPATHY CAN PROGRESS RAPIDLY DURING PREGNANCY (especially with abrupt tightening of glycaemic control) — arrange fundus examination each trimester, and treat proliferative disease with laser BEFORE conception.**
- **Preconception care is the single most valuable intervention: optimise HbA1c (<6.5%), start 5 mg FOLIC ACID, STOP ACE inhibitors/ARBs and STATINS, screen for retinopathy, nephropathy and thyroid disease, and switch oral agents to insulin where appropriate.**
- **Insulin requirements FALL in the first trimester, RISE from the second, and DROP SHARPLY after delivery — reduce the dose immediately postpartum.**
- **The modern classification is simply GESTATIONAL versus PRE-GESTATIONAL (type 1/type 2) diabetes, with the ACOG 1986 modification of White's system.**
- **Only pre-gestational diabetes causes congenital malformations; gestational diabetes appears too late in gestation to do so.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "All managed with insulin EXCEPT" → **class A1**.

**REMEMBER:** *A1 is diet, everything else is insulin — and the letters track how long the diabetes has been damaging vessels.*

`Q11119`

---

## 77. Amniotic Fluid pH and the Diagnosis of Rupture of Membranes

**PEARL:** **Amniotic fluid pH is 7.1–7.3 (ALKALINE); normal vaginal secretions are ACIDIC at 4.5–5.5.** When membranes rupture, the vaginal pH rises to **6.0–6.5**, turning **NITRAZINE paper from yellow to BLUE** — the basis of the nitrazine test.

**UNDERSTAND:** The vagina is kept acidic by **lactobacilli fermenting glycogen to lactic acid**, and that acidity is what the test measures the loss of. This immediately predicts the **FALSE POSITIVES: anything alkaline in the vagina — BLOOD, SEMEN, urine, antiseptics, soap, and BACTERIAL VAGINOSIS (in which lactobacilli are depleted and the pH is already >4.5).** False negatives occur with prolonged leakage and minimal residual fluid.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The diagnosis of PROM is made by STERILE SPECULUM EXAMINATION showing liquor pooling in the posterior fornix — the most reliable sign. DIGITAL VAGINAL EXAMINATION MUST BE AVOIDED (it introduces infection and shortens the latency period) unless the woman is in labour.**
- **FERNING: amniotic fluid dried on a slide crystallises in an arborising pattern (high sodium chloride and protein). Blood and meconium reduce its reliability.** **Modern tests are the IGFBP-1 (Actim PROM) and PAMG-1 (AmniSure) immunoassays**, which are unaffected by blood, semen or urine.
- **PROM = rupture before labour at any gestation; PPROM = PRETERM prelabour rupture, before 37 weeks. The dominant risk in both is ASCENDING INFECTION (chorioamnionitis) and, in PPROM, prematurity plus cord prolapse and pulmonary hypoplasia with prolonged oligohydramnios.**
- **PPROM management: ADMIT; give ANTENATAL CORTICOSTEROIDS; give LATENCY ANTIBIOTICS (erythromycin — NOT co-amoxiclav, which increases NECROTISING ENTEROCOLITIS, from the ORACLE trial); magnesium sulphate for neuroprotection below 32 weeks; monitor for chorioamnionitis; and DELIVER AT 34–37 WEEKS or sooner if infection supervenes. TOCOLYSIS IS GENERALLY NOT USED.**
- **CHORIOAMNIONITIS: maternal fever, maternal and fetal TACHYCARDIA, uterine tenderness, offensive liquor and leucocytosis — treat with broad-spectrum antibiotics and DELIVER, regardless of gestation.**
- **At term with PROM, INDUCTION (rather than expectant management) reduces chorioamnionitis without increasing caesarean rates (the TERMPROM trial).**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Number recall → **7.1–7.3**, and the nitrazine false-positive list.

**REMEMBER:** *The vagina is acid and the liquor is alkaline — but so are blood, semen and bacterial vaginosis.*

`Q11123`

---

## 78. Eponymous Obstetric Signs

**PEARL:** **"PLACENTAL SIGN" = SPOTTING at the expected date of the period in the EARLY MONTHS of pregnancy**, occurring until the **decidua capsularis fuses with the decidua parietalis at ~12 weeks**, obliterating the uterine cavity. **STALLWORTHY'S SIGN = slowing of the FETAL HEART RATE when the head is pressed into the pelvis, recovering on release — suggesting POSTERIOR PLACENTA PRAEVIA.** **Permanent CORD LENGTHENING and a slight GUSH OF BLOOD are signs of PLACENTAL SEPARATION in the third stage.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The placental sign exists because, until about 12 weeks, **the growing sac has not yet filled the uterine cavity**, so a potential space remains between the decidua capsularis (over the sac) and the decidua parietalis (lining the rest of the uterus). **Cyclical hormonal withdrawal can still cause shedding in that space.** Once the two fuse, the cavity is obliterated and the bleeding stops — a neat anatomical explanation for a symptom that otherwise alarms both patient and doctor.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Signs of placental separation in the third stage: the uterus becomes FIRM, GLOBULAR AND BALLOTTABLE and rises up; there is PERMANENT LENGTHENING OF THE CORD; and there is a SUDDEN GUSH OF BLOOD.** **NEVER apply cord traction before separation — it risks UTERINE INVERSION.**
- **Modes of placental separation: SCHULTZE (central separation — the shiny fetal surface appears first, with the blood retained behind the placenta) and MATTHEWS DUNCAN (marginal separation — the maternal surface appears first, with visible bleeding throughout).**
- **Other classic signs: HEGAR'S SIGN (softening of the isthmus at 6–10 weeks), GOODELL'S (cervical softening), CHADWICK/JACQUEMIER'S (bluish vaginal discoloration), OSIANDER'S (pulsation in the lateral fornices), PALMER'S (rhythmic uterine contractions on bimanual examination), and PISKACEK'S (asymmetrical uterine enlargement at the implantation site).**
- **BRAXTON HICKS contractions are painless, irregular, non-progressive contractions that do not dilate the cervix.**
- **QUICKENING is felt at ~18–20 weeks in a primigravida and ~16–18 weeks in a multigravida.**
- **In the first trimester, ANY bleeding requires exclusion of ECTOPIC PREGNANCY and MISCARRIAGE before attributing it to the placental sign.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Definition matching — **placental sign = cyclical spotting in early pregnancy**.

**REMEMBER:** *Until the decidua fuse at twelve weeks, there is still a space in which she can bleed on time.*

`Q11126`

---

## 79. Uterine Involution

**PEARL:** **Immediately after delivery the uterus weighs ~1000 g and corresponds to a 20-WEEK size, lying just below the umbilicus. At 1 WEEK it weighs 500 g; at 2 WEEKS it becomes a PELVIC ORGAN (300 g, no longer palpable abdominally); at 4 WEEKS involution is clinically complete (100 g); and by 6–8 WEEKS it returns to its pre-pregnant weight of ~60 g.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Involution occurs by **a reduction in the SIZE of individual myocytes (autolysis and protein catabolism), not by a fall in their NUMBER** — the extra muscle cells acquired in pregnancy shrink rather than disappear. This is why involution is **rapid, complete and repeatable across pregnancies**, and why it is accelerated by **breastfeeding (oxytocin release)** and delayed by retained products, infection, fibroids and a full bladder.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **SUBINVOLUTION — a uterus that is larger and softer than expected, with persistent or offensive lochia — is caused by RETAINED PRODUCTS OF CONCEPTION, ENDOMETRITIS, fibroids, a full bladder or a retroverted uterus. It is a leading cause of SECONDARY POSTPARTUM HAEMORRHAGE (24 hours to 12 weeks), managed with antibiotics, uterotonics and evacuation if products are retained.**
- **LOCHIA: RUBRA (red, days 1–4), SEROSA (pale/pinkish, days 5–9), ALBA (whitish, days 10–15). An OFFENSIVE odour means infection; persistent rubra means retained products.**
- **AFTER-PAINS — painful uterine contractions during breastfeeding — are commoner in MULTIPARAE and are due to oxytocin release.**
- **Other puerperal changes: a diuresis in the first week (clearing the pregnancy fluid load), a physiological leucocytosis, and a persisting HYPERCOAGULABLE state making the puerperium the peak time for VTE.**
- **The cervix regains its shape within days but the external os remains a TRANSVERSE SLIT permanently after vaginal delivery — the marker of parity.**
- **Routine postnatal care: check involution, lochia, perineum, breasts, blood pressure and mood; screen for POSTNATAL DEPRESSION; provide contraception; and continue iron and calcium.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Uterus becomes a pelvic organ at" → **2 weeks**; "immediately after delivery corresponds to" → **20 weeks**.

**REMEMBER:** *Twenty weeks at delivery, pelvic by two weeks, normal by six.*

`Q11127, Q11395`

---

## 80. Ventouse versus Forceps

**PEARL:** **VENTOUSE IS ABSOLUTELY CONTRAINDICATED in PRETERM (<34 weeks — risk of intracranial and subgaleal haemorrhage in a soft skull), FACE PRESENTATION, the AFTER-COMING HEAD OF A BREECH, and INTRAUTERINE FETAL DEATH** (and in suspected fetal bleeding disorders). **FORCEPS is the instrument of choice in all of these.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The vacuum works by **applying traction to the fetal SCALP**, so it requires a **firm, ossified skull and a vertex to grip**. A preterm skull is too compressible; a face has no suitable application point; and the after-coming head of a breech needs **controlled, immediate delivery with flexion (PIPER FORCEPS)**, not the gradual traction and possible cup detachment of a ventouse. Forceps, by contrast, **grip the fetal BONES and transmit force to the skull as a whole.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Prerequisites for ANY instrumental delivery: FULLY DILATED cervix, RUPTURED membranes, an ENGAGED head with the position and station known, an empty bladder, adequate analgesia, no cephalopelvic disproportion, and a prepared plan for failure. NEVER apply an instrument to an unengaged head.**
- **Ventouse advantages: less maternal trauma, less analgesia needed, allows AUTO-ROTATION. Disadvantages: higher failure rate, CEPHALHAEMATOMA, SUBGALEAL HAEMORRHAGE (potentially life-threatening — the subgaleal space can hold the neonate's entire blood volume), and retinal haemorrhage. Time and pull limits: abandon after ~3 pulls, 2 cup detachments or 20 minutes.**
- **Forceps advantages: higher success rate, faster, usable for face and after-coming head, and it can rotate (KIELLAND'S). Disadvantages: more maternal trauma (third/fourth-degree tears, vaginal lacerations) and facial nerve palsy.**
- **Forceps types: SIMPSON/Anderson (outlet and low), Wrigley's (outlet, and for caesarean), KIELLAND'S (rotational, minimal pelvic curve), PIPER (after-coming head of the breech).**
- **Indications: prolonged or arrested second stage, maternal exhaustion, fetal distress, and to SHORTEN the second stage in maternal cardiac or neurological disease, severe pre-eclampsia or previous caesarean.**
- **SEQUENTIAL USE of ventouse followed by forceps markedly increases neonatal trauma and should be avoided.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Ventouse is contraindicated in" → **preterm**.

**REMEMBER:** *The cup needs a hard vertex to hold — preterm, face and after-coming heads belong to forceps.*

`Q11381`

---

## 81. Sheehan Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Postpartum haemorrhage followed by HYPOTENSION unresponsive to fluids, TACHYCARDIA, HYPOGLYCAEMIA, headache, visual disturbance and collapse = SHEEHAN SYNDROME (postpartum pituitary necrosis).** The life-saving treatment is **GLUCOCORTICOIDS (hydrocortisone) FIRST**, followed by thyroxine and other replacement.

**UNDERSTAND:** The anterior pituitary **doubles in size in pregnancy (lactotroph hyperplasia) while its blood supply — the low-pressure hypophyseal PORTAL system — does not keep pace**, leaving it uniquely vulnerable to hypotension. Severe haemorrhage causes **infarction of the anterior lobe**, sparing the posterior lobe (which has a direct arterial supply) — so **diabetes insipidus is uncommon**. Cortisol deficiency explains the whole acute picture: **fluid- and pressor-resistant hypotension, hypoglycaemia and hyponatraemia.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **STEROIDS MUST BE GIVEN BEFORE THYROXINE** — thyroxine accelerates cortisol metabolism and can precipitate an adrenal crisis in an unrecognised cortisol-deficient patient.
- **The classic EARLIEST and most characteristic sign is FAILURE OF LACTATION (prolactin deficiency), followed by failure of resumption of menses, loss of axillary and pubic hair, fatigue, cold intolerance and weight gain.** Presentation may be **acute (as here) or insidious over months to years**.
- **Order of hormone loss with progressive pituitary damage: GH → FSH/LH → TSH → ACTH → prolactin ("GO LOOK FOR THE ADENOMA").** In Sheehan's, prolactin is affected early because lactotrophs are the hyperplastic, most vulnerable cells.
- **Diagnosis: low target hormones with INAPPROPRIATELY LOW or normal trophic hormones (low free T4 with a low/normal TSH; low cortisol with a low/normal ACTH); MRI shows an EMPTY SELLA in the chronic phase.**
- **Distinguish LYMPHOCYTIC HYPOPHYSITIS — autoimmune, also presents in late pregnancy or postpartum with headache and hypopituitarism, but often with a pituitary MASS and preserved lactation, and it may respond to steroids.**
- **Prevention is simply the prevention and prompt treatment of postpartum haemorrhage — which is why Sheehan syndrome remains common in India and is rare where obstetric haemorrhage is well managed.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** PPH then shock, hypoglycaemia and visual symptoms → **give steroids**.

**REMEMBER:** *The pituitary outgrows its blood supply in pregnancy — bleed enough and it infarcts, so give hydrocortisone first.*

`Q11382`

---

## 82. The MTP Act (as amended, 2021)

**PEARL:** **Up to 20 WEEKS — ONE registered medical practitioner's opinion. 20 to 24 WEEKS — TWO practitioners' opinions, and ONLY for SPECIFIED CATEGORIES (survivors of rape/incest, minors, women with a change in marital status during pregnancy — widowhood or divorce, women with disability, mentally ill women, fetal malformation, and humanitarian/disaster settings). BEYOND 24 WEEKS — only for SUBSTANTIAL FETAL ABNORMALITY, on the approval of a STATE MEDICAL BOARD.** **FAILURE OF CONTRACEPTION is a valid indication ONLY UP TO 20 WEEKS** — and it now applies to **any woman and her partner**, not only married couples.

**UNDERSTAND:** The gestational thresholds track **fetal viability and procedural risk**, while the "specified categories" exist because the 2021 amendment was designed to **extend access to women whose circumstances change late or who discover problems late**, without opening a general late-termination provision. This is exactly why **contraceptive failure — a circumstance known from the outset — did NOT get the extended limit.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **CONSENT: the WOMAN'S OWN written consent alone is sufficient — the HUSBAND'S CONSENT IS NOT REQUIRED. For a MINOR (<18 years) or a MENTALLY ILL/mentally unsound woman, the consent of the GUARDIAN in writing is required.** **Confidentiality is statutory** — the name and particulars must not be revealed except to a person authorised by law (revealing them is a punishable offence).
- **In a MINOR, the POCSO Act mandates reporting of sexual offences, though the identity may be withheld from the police report at the woman's request under the amended rules.**
- **Grounds for termination: risk to the woman's LIFE, risk of grave injury to her PHYSICAL OR MENTAL HEALTH, SUBSTANTIAL FETAL ABNORMALITY, pregnancy from RAPE (presumed to cause grave mental injury), and CONTRACEPTIVE FAILURE.**
- **Methods: FIRST TRIMESTER — MEDICAL (MIFEPRISTONE 200 mg followed 24–48 hours later by MISOPROSTOL, approved up to 9 weeks in India) or SURGICAL (MANUAL/electric VACUUM ASPIRATION, or dilatation and evacuation). SECOND TRIMESTER — mifepristone plus misoprostol, dilatation and evacuation, or (historically) EXTRA-AMNIOTIC ETHACRIDINE LACTATE and intra-amniotic hypertonic saline — the last of which are second-trimester methods only and are now rarely used.**
- **The procedure must be done in an APPROVED facility by a registered practitioner with prescribed training; contraception must be offered afterwards, and anti-D given to Rh-negative women.**
- **The PCPNDT ACT is separate and prohibits SEX DETERMINATION — the two laws must not be confused.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** MTP at 22 weeks for contraceptive failure → **it cannot be done**; consent for a mentally unsound woman → **legal guardian**.

**REMEMBER:** *Twenty weeks for anyone, twenty-four for the special categories, and only a Medical Board beyond that.*

`Q11384, Q11388, Q11394`

---

## 83. Asherman's Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Intrauterine adhesions following SHARP CURETTAGE (especially on a recently pregnant uterus) or genital TUBERCULOSIS, presenting with HYPOMENORRHOEA, SECONDARY AMENORRHOEA, recurrent miscarriage and SECONDARY INFERTILITY = ASHERMAN'S SYNDROME.** **HYSTEROSCOPY is both the investigation of choice and the treatment (HYSTEROSCOPIC ADHESIOLYSIS).** On **HSG the adhesions appear as irregular filling defects — a "MOTH-EATEN" cavity.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The lesion is **destruction of the BASALIS layer of the endometrium**, which is the regenerative layer — so the functionalis can no longer be rebuilt each cycle and the raw walls adhere. This explains everything: **amenorrhoea despite normal ovarian function and normal hormones, a NEGATIVE PROGESTERONE CHALLENGE TEST (there is no endometrium to shed), and infertility from failure of implantation.** It also explains why curettage on a soft, recently pregnant uterus is the classic cause.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **In the amenorrhoea work-up, Asherman's is the outflow/end-organ cause: gonadotrophins are NORMAL, oestrogen is normal, and there is NO withdrawal bleed even after oestrogen PLUS progesterone.**
- **Treatment: hysteroscopic adhesiolysis, followed by measures to prevent re-formation — an intrauterine balloon, catheter or IUCD, and CYCLICAL OESTROGEN to regenerate the endometrium; a second-look hysteroscopy is often needed. Reproductive outcome depends on the severity of the original damage.**
- **GENITAL TUBERCULOSIS is a major cause in India — it typically affects the FALLOPIAN TUBES FIRST (the commonest site, causing a "beaded" tube and a "golf-club" appearance on HSG) and then the endometrium, causing infertility, hypomenorrhoea and amenorrhoea. Diagnose by endometrial biopsy/PCR of premenstrual endometrial sampling; treat with full ATT, but tubal damage is usually irreversible and ART is needed.**
- **Other hysteroscopic indications: uterine SEPTUM resection, submucous fibroid resection, ENDOMETRIAL POLYPECTOMY, retained IUCD retrieval, endometrial ablation, and hysteroscopic tubal occlusion. LAPAROSCOPY, not hysteroscopy, is used for tubal ligation and for SUBSEROSAL fibroids.**
- **Prevention: use SUCTION EVACUATION rather than sharp curettage, and avoid over-vigorous curettage of a recently pregnant uterus.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Hysteroscopy can be done in" → **Asherman's syndrome**.

**REMEMBER:** *Scrape away the basalis and the cavity sticks shut — no endometrium, no bleed, no implantation.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/08/10/screenshot-2024-08-10-175609.png)

`Q11385`

## 84. Vaginal Discharge — Reading the Slide

**PEARL:** **FROTHY, FOUL-SMELLING, YELLOW-GREEN discharge with intense itching and a "STRAWBERRY CERVIX" (punctate haemorrhages) = *TRICHOMONAS VAGINALIS*** — a flagellated protozoan seen as **jerky, motile organisms on a saline wet mount**, with vaginal pH **>4.5**. Treat with **METRONIDAZOLE 2 g single dose, AND TREAT THE PARTNER** (it is a sexually transmitted infection).

**UNDERSTAND:** The three common causes of discharge are separated by **pH and microscopy**, and the reason is microbiological: **candidiasis leaves the lactobacilli intact so the pH stays ACIDIC (<4.5), whereas bacterial vaginosis and trichomoniasis both DISPLACE the lactobacilli and raise the pH above 4.5.** The strawberry cervix reflects **inflammation of the cervical epithelium** — trichomonas is the only one of the three that is genuinely inflammatory (hence itching, dysuria and dyspareunia).

**MUST KNOW:**
- **BACTERIAL VAGINOSIS: thin, homogeneous, GREY-WHITE discharge with a FISHY odour, pH >4.5, "CLUE CELLS" on microscopy, and a POSITIVE WHIFF (AMSEL) TEST with 10% KOH — with FEW OR NO pus cells and NO inflammation (hence "vaginosis", not "vaginitis"). It is an overgrowth of *Gardnerella*, anaerobes and *Mobiluncus* with LOSS OF LACTOBACILLI. Treat with metronidazole; PARTNER TREATMENT IS NOT REQUIRED. It is associated with PRETERM LABOUR, PROM and postpartum endometritis.**
- **VULVOVAGINAL CANDIDIASIS: thick, curdy, WHITE "cottage-cheese" discharge adherent to the vaginal wall, intense itching and erythema, pH NORMAL (<4.5), pseudohyphae and budding yeasts on KOH. Predisposed by pregnancy, DIABETES, antibiotics, steroids and immunosuppression. Treat with topical or oral azoles (fluconazole is avoided in the first trimester); PARTNER TREATMENT NOT REQUIRED.**
- **TRICHOMONAS in pregnancy is associated with preterm birth and low birth weight, and it increases HIV transmission — always offer HIV and other STI screening, and treat the partner.**
- **Metronidazole is safe in pregnancy (avoid the 2 g single dose in the first trimester where possible) — and warn about the DISULFIRAM-LIKE REACTION with alcohol.**
- **PHYSIOLOGICAL leucorrhoea is clear/white, non-offensive and non-irritant, increases in pregnancy and at midcycle, and needs no treatment.**
- **Mucopurulent CERVICITIS with contact bleeding suggests *CHLAMYDIA* or *GONORRHOEA* — test by NAAT and treat both partners.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Frothy discharge + strawberry cervix → ***Trichomonas vaginalis***.

**REMEMBER:** *Curdy and acid is candida; grey and fishy is vaginosis; frothy, itchy and strawberry is trichomonas.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/09/14/2_page_1.jpg)

`Q11387`

---

## 85. Occipitoposterior Position

**PEARL:** A primigravida in early labour with **adequate contractions, a GYNECOID pelvis and a RIGHT OCCIPITOPOSTERIOR vertex should be managed EXPECTANTLY — NORMAL VAGINAL DELIVERY is anticipated.** About **90% of occipitoposterior positions ROTATE SPONTANEOUSLY through 135° to occipitoanterior** when the pelvis is favourable and the contractions are good.

**UNDERSTAND:** Occipitoposterior is a **position, not an abnormality**. The determinants of outcome are the **three Ps**: with a **gynecoid pelvis (passage), good contractions (powers) and a normally flexed, normally sized head (passenger)**, the pelvic floor gutter will rotate the occiput forwards. Intervening early with instruments or caesarean, before giving labour a chance, is the error the question tests.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Clinical features: a LONG, PAINFUL labour with prominent BACKACHE, early rupture of membranes, a tendency to bear down BEFORE full dilatation (the occiput presses on the rectum), slow descent, and a flat or saucer-shaped lower abdomen with the fetal limbs easily palpable anteriorly. On vaginal examination the ANTERIOR FONTANELLE is felt anteriorly (a deflexed head).**
- **Possible outcomes: (1) LONG ANTERIOR ROTATION through 135° to occipitoanterior — the commonest and most favourable; (2) SHORT POSTERIOR rotation to direct occipitoposterior, delivering "FACE TO PUBIS" (a bigger perineal stretch and more tears); (3) DEEP TRANSVERSE ARREST; (4) NON-ROTATION with arrest, requiring intervention.**
- **Management: adequate ANALGESIA (epidural), HYDRATION, oxytocin augmentation if contractions are inadequate, and PATIENCE — plus an upright or all-fours maternal position to encourage rotation. If the second stage arrests: MANUAL ROTATION plus direct forceps, ROTATIONAL FORCEPS (Kielland's), or VENTOUSE with auto-rotation; caesarean if the pelvis is inadequate or the attempt fails.**
- **Risk factors: ANDROID and ANTHROPOID pelves (the anthropoid pelvis often delivers DIRECT OCCIPITOPOSTERIOR successfully), a deflexed head, and epidural analgesia (a lax pelvic floor).**
- **Complications: prolonged labour, maternal exhaustion, third/fourth-degree perineal tears, PPH, and fetal hypoxia.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Early labour, gynecoid pelvis, ROP, adequate contractions → **allow normal vaginal delivery**.

**REMEMBER:** *Occipitoposterior is a position, not a diagnosis — a good pelvis and good contractions will rotate it.*

`Q11386`

---

## 86. A Contradiction Worth Knowing — Evaluating Amenorrhoea with High FSH/LH

**PEARL:** The SAME stem — **a 25-year-old with 4 months of amenorrhoea, ELEVATED FSH and LH, and normal thyroid function** — has been keyed **BOTH ways** in the source papers: as **"estimate serum oestradiol"** (INI-CET 2017 as published in one key) and as **"give progesterone for 10 days and look for withdrawal bleeding"** (the same question in a repeat paper). **Know both lines of reasoning.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The **defensible physiology favours OESTRADIOL**: raised gonadotrophins already localise the lesion to the ovary (**hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism = premature ovarian insufficiency**), and the progesterone challenge test is designed to assess **oestrogen status and outflow patency in a woman whose gonadotrophins are NORMAL** (i.e. suspected anovulation/PCOS). Applying a progesterone challenge to a woman with clearly raised FSH adds little — she will usually NOT bleed, because she is already hypo-oestrogenic. The **alternative reasoning** treats the progesterone challenge as the standard FIRST step in ANY secondary amenorrhoea once pregnancy is excluded, which is how many Indian textbooks present the algorithm.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The classification that actually matters: HIGH FSH/LH → ovarian failure (POI, Turner mosaicism, gonadal dysgenesis, chemotherapy/radiotherapy, autoimmune, FMR1 premutation, galactosaemia). LOW FSH/LH → hypothalamic-pituitary (stress, weight loss, excessive exercise, hyperprolactinaemia, Sheehan's, Kallmann's, tumours). NORMAL FSH/LH → PCOS or an outflow problem (Asherman's, cervical stenosis).**
- **RAISED FSH AND LH RELIABLY EXCLUDE PREGNANCY**, because pregnancy suppresses both — so a urine pregnancy test adds nothing once they are known to be high (this is why option "do a UPT" is wrong in both versions).
- **Confirm POI with TWO FSH measurements >25–40 IU/L, 4–6 weeks apart, plus a low oestradiol and low AMH; then investigate the cause with KARYOTYPE, FMR1 premutation testing, and adrenal/thyroid autoantibodies.**
- **Treat POI with HORMONE REPLACEMENT until the average age of menopause** — for bone and cardiovascular protection, not merely symptom relief. **Spontaneous ovulation still occurs in 5–10%, so contraception is needed if pregnancy is not desired; donor oocyte IVF is the fertility option.**
- **If it reappears in an exam, choose the option that matches the WORDING: if the stem emphasises the raised gonadotrophins, the intended answer is usually the OVARIAN work-up (oestradiol/karyotype); if it asks for the "first step in evaluating secondary amenorrhoea", it is the PROGESTERONE CHALLENGE.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** A genuine split key — read the stem's emphasis before answering.

**REMEMBER:** *High FSH already tells you the ovary has failed — the progesterone challenge is a test for the woman whose gonadotrophins are normal.*

`Q9111, Q11396`

---

## 87. Treating Gestational Trophoblastic Neoplasia by Risk Score

**PEARL:** Post-molar choriocarcinoma with **LUNG ("CANNONBALL") METASTASES and persistently raised hCG** is staged and scored by the **FIGO/WHO PROGNOSTIC SCORE: ≤6 = LOW RISK → SINGLE-AGENT CHEMOTHERAPY, classically MULTI-DOSE METHOTREXATE (days 1, 3, 5, 7) ALTERNATING WITH FOLINIC ACID RESCUE (days 2, 4, 6, 8), or actinomycin-D. ≥7 = HIGH RISK → COMBINATION EMA-CO.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Folinic acid **rescues normal cells from methotrexate's block of dihydrofolate reductase without rescuing the tumour**, allowing higher, more effective methotrexate exposure with far less mucositis and myelosuppression. The whole treatment philosophy of GTN rests on one fact: **trophoblastic tumours are exquisitely chemosensitive and CURABLE EVEN WITH METASTASES**, so the aim is cure with **fertility preserved** — hysterectomy is not required.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The WHO score counts: AGE, ANTECEDENT PREGNANCY (mole < abortion < term), INTERVAL from that pregnancy, pre-treatment hCG, largest tumour size, SITE of metastases (lung scores least; BRAIN and LIVER score most), NUMBER of metastases, and previous failed chemotherapy.**
- **The LUNG is the commonest metastatic site ("cannonball" shadows); then vagina, brain and liver. STAGE: I confined to the uterus; II genital tract spread; III LUNG; IV all other sites (the worst).**
- **Continue chemotherapy until hCG is normal, then give 2–3 CONSOLIDATION COURSES to prevent relapse, and follow hCG monthly for 12 months with STRICT CONTRACEPTION throughout.**
- **BRAIN metastases require higher-dose methotrexate with intrathecal therapy and/or whole-brain radiotherapy; heavy tumour burden risks HAEMORRHAGE at the start of chemotherapy.**
- **PLACENTAL SITE TROPHOBLASTIC TUMOUR is the exception: LOW hCG (raised hPL), CHEMORESISTANT, and treated by HYSTERECTOMY.**
- **Choriocarcinoma may follow a molar pregnancy (~50%), a normal term pregnancy (~25%), or an abortion/ectopic — so any woman with abnormal bleeding or metastatic disease of unknown origin in the reproductive years must have an hCG measured.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Low-risk metastatic GTN → **methotrexate WITH folinic acid** (not methotrexate alone, not EMA-CO).

**REMEMBER:** *Score it first: six or less is one drug with folinic rescue; seven or more is EMA-CO.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/09/15/1.jpg)

`Q11397`

---

## 88. Diabetes Management Around Delivery

**PEARL:** **ELECTIVE CAESAREAN DOES reduce brachial plexus injury in the macrosomic infant of a diabetic mother — and is recommended at an estimated fetal weight ≥4.5 kg in diabetics (≥5 kg in non-diabetics).** For a planned induction, **give the usual NIGHT dose of intermediate-acting insulin and WITHHOLD THE MORNING DOSE**; in active labour, run an **insulin–dextrose infusion targeting capillary glucose ~70–110 mg/dL.** Antenatal targets: **fasting <95, 1-hour postprandial <140, 2-hour postprandial <120 mg/dL.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Labour is **profound physical exercise**, so glucose consumption rises steeply and insulin requirements fall — while at the same time **maternal hyperglycaemia in labour directly drives fetal hyperinsulinaemia and therefore NEONATAL HYPOGLYCAEMIA.** The regimen therefore aims for a narrow band: **enough glucose to fuel labour, and enough insulin to keep the fetus from being hyperinsulinaemic at the moment of birth.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **INSULIN REQUIREMENTS FALL DRAMATICALLY IMMEDIATELY AFTER DELIVERY OF THE PLACENTA — cut the dose to roughly the pre-pregnancy amount (or stop insulin altogether in gestational diabetes) to avoid hypoglycaemia.**
- **Diabetic macrosomia is TRUNCAL and shoulder-predominant, which is why shoulder dystocia risk at a given birth weight is HIGHER in diabetics than in non-diabetics — the basis for the lower caesarean threshold.**
- **Sonographic estimates of fetal weight carry a ±10–15% error, so the decision must be clinical as well as sonographic; caesarean for suspected macrosomia prevents some injuries but at the cost of many unnecessary operations.**
- **Neonatal care: early feeding, capillary glucose monitoring, and screening for hypocalcaemia, polycythaemia, hyperbilirubinaemia and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.**
- **Postpartum: encourage breastfeeding (it lowers the later risk of type 2 diabetes in both mother and child), review contraception, and arrange a 75 g OGTT at 6–12 weeks in gestational diabetes.**
- **METFORMIN and insulin are both acceptable in lactation.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "False regarding management" → **elective caesarean has no role in reducing brachial plexus injury** (it does).

**REMEMBER:** *Night insulin yes, morning insulin no — and the requirement collapses the moment the placenta is out.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/09/27/untitled-236.jpg)

`Q11398`

## 89. Missed Combined Oral Contraceptive Pills

**PEARL:** **ONE OR TWO missed pills (a delay of >24–48 hours) at any time: TAKE THE MISSED PILL(S) AT ONCE — up to 2 pills on the same day — and continue the pack; no extra precaution is needed.** **THREE OR MORE missed pills: take the most recent pill, USE BARRIER PROTECTION FOR 7 DAYS, and consider emergency contraception; if they were missed in the THIRD WEEK, OMIT THE HORMONE-FREE INTERVAL and START A NEW PACK IMMEDIATELY.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The entire rule set exists to protect **the 7-day rule of ovarian suppression** — 7 consecutive active pills are needed to suppress follicular development reliably, and the **pill-free week is the point of greatest vulnerability** because the ovary is already 7 days into recovery. So **missing pills adjacent to the hormone-free interval (week 1 or week 3) is the dangerous situation**, and the remedy is to eliminate that interval.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Missing pills in the FIRST WEEK with unprotected intercourse in the preceding 5 days is the situation where EMERGENCY CONTRACEPTION is most clearly indicated.** Missing pills in the second week is the least risky.
- **The rules differ for PROGESTOGEN-ONLY PILLS, which have a much narrower window: a delay of >3 HOURS (traditional POPs) or >12 hours (desogestrel) means taking the pill at once plus BARRIER PROTECTION FOR 48 HOURS.**
- **Vomiting within 2–3 hours or severe diarrhoea is treated as a missed pill.**
- **ENZYME-INDUCING DRUGS (rifampicin, phenytoin, carbamazepine, St John's wort) reduce efficacy — use an alternative method.**
- **EXTENDED or CONTINUOUS regimens (skipping the hormone-free interval deliberately) are safe and are used for endometriosis, menorrhagia, dysmenorrhoea, PMS and menstrual migraine.**
- **A woman starting COCs on day 1–5 of the cycle needs no additional protection; starting later ("quick start") requires 7 days of barrier protection.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Two pills missed in the third week → **take 2 pills the same day and continue**.

**REMEMBER:** *Seven active pills protect you — so never let a gap fall next to the pill-free week.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/06/07/screenshot-2024-06-07-122802.png)

`Q11399`

## 90. Magnesium Sulphate — What It Does and Does Not Do

**PEARL:** **MAGNESIUM DOES NOT LOWER BLOOD PRESSURE — it is an ANTICONVULSANT, not an antihypertensive**, and antihypertensives must be given separately. **STOP or withhold it if URINE OUTPUT is <30 mL/hour (i.e. <60 mL in 4 hours), if the RESPIRATORY RATE is <12–16/min, or if the PATELLAR REFLEX IS ABSENT.** It **relaxes the uterus in a DOSE-DEPENDENT manner**, and in RENAL FAILURE the **LOADING dose is unchanged but the MAINTENANCE dose must be reduced.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The loading dose is determined by the **volume of distribution** (unchanged by renal function), while the maintenance dose is determined by **CLEARANCE — and magnesium is cleared entirely by the kidney with no hepatic metabolism.** This is why oliguria is the single most dangerous situation on magnesium therapy: the drug simply accumulates. Its anticonvulsant action is **cerebral vasodilatation, NMDA receptor blockade and membrane stabilisation** — none of which produce a useful antihypertensive effect.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Bedside monitoring before every dose: DEEP TENDON REFLEXES, RESPIRATORY RATE, URINE OUTPUT — reflexes go first (~10 mEq/L), respiration next (~12), cardiac arrest last (~25–30). Antidote: CALCIUM GLUCONATE 1 g IV.**
- **Because magnesium relaxes the uterus, it may contribute to UTERINE ATONY and PPH after delivery — anticipate it.**
- **Other uses: FETAL NEUROPROTECTION before preterm birth <32 weeks, as a tocolytic, and in severe asthma.**
- **AVOID in MYASTHENIA GRAVIS (precipitates crisis); use caution with CALCIUM CHANNEL BLOCKERS (profound hypotension) and with non-depolarising neuromuscular blockers (potentiation).**
- **Pre-eclampsia pathogenesis for the "theories" question: there is REDUCED PROSTACYCLIN (PGI2, a vasodilator and antiplatelet agent) and INCREASED THROMBOXANE A2 (a vasoconstrictor and platelet aggregator) — so the PROSTACYCLIN:THROMBOXANE RATIO FALLS, not rises. This imbalance is precisely why LOW-DOSE ASPIRIN works: it preferentially inhibits platelet thromboxane while sparing endothelial prostacyclin.**
- **The other accepted mechanisms: abnormal trophoblastic invasion, endothelial dysfunction with REDUCED NITRIC OXIDE, an ANTI-ANGIOGENIC state (raised sFlt-1 and soluble endoglin, reduced PlGF and VEGF), INCREASED VASCULAR SENSITIVITY TO ANGIOTENSIN II with reduced angiotensinase, immunological maladaptation, and genetic factors.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Stop MgSO4 when" → **urine output 60 mL in 4 hours**; "false about MgSO4" → **it lowers blood pressure**.

**REMEMBER:** *Magnesium stops fits, relaxes the uterus and leaves by the kidney — it never treats the blood pressure.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/09/27/untitled-237.jpg)

`Q11400, Q11401, Q11414`

## 91. Acute Pyelonephritis in Pregnancy

**PEARL:** Management is **ADMISSION, urine and blood CULTURES, IV ANTIBIOTICS (a third-generation cephalosporin), IV CRYSTALLOIDS maintaining a urine output ≥50 mL/hour, and a switch to ORAL antibiotics once AFEBRILE FOR 24–48 HOURS.** **The total course is 10–14 DAYS (not 5), followed by SUPPRESSIVE PROPHYLAXIS (nitrofurantoin at night) FOR THE REST OF PREGNANCY**, with monthly urine cultures.

**UNDERSTAND:** Pregnancy converts a routine infection into a systemic emergency because of **physiological hydroureter and stasis (progesterone plus mechanical compression, worse on the RIGHT)**, and because **endotoxin release causes ENDOTHELIAL INJURY** — hence the feared complications of **ARDS (in up to 10%), septic shock, haemolysis and preterm labour.** That is why hospitalisation and generous hydration with a measured urine output are mandatory rather than optional.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **It is the commonest serious medical complication of pregnancy, occurs most often in the SECOND TRIMESTER, is RIGHT-SIDED in ~half of cases, and *E. COLI* causes ~70–80%.**
- **PREVENTION IS THE POINT: SCREEN EVERY PREGNANT WOMAN FOR ASYMPTOMATIC BACTERIURIA (urine culture at the first antenatal visit) AND TREAT IT — untreated, 20–30% progress to pyelonephritis; treatment reduces both pyelonephritis and preterm birth. This is one of the few settings where asymptomatic bacteriuria is treated.**
- **Safe antibiotics in pregnancy: PENICILLINS, CEPHALOSPORINS, nitrofurantoin (AVOID at term/in G6PD deficiency — haemolysis and neonatal jaundice), fosfomycin, and erythromycin. AVOID: FLUOROQUINOLONES (cartilage), TETRACYCLINES (teeth and bone), cotrimoxazole (first trimester — folate antagonism; and at term — kernicterus), and aminoglycosides (ototoxicity) unless essential.**
- **Failure to improve within 48–72 hours means imaging for OBSTRUCTION, a renal or perinephric ABSCESS, or a resistant organism — ultrasound first.**
- **Watch for the complications: monitor respiratory rate and oxygen saturation (ARDS is often precipitated by over-vigorous fluids plus endotoxin), and for uterine contractions.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Incorrect regarding management" → **discharge after 72 hours afebrile with only 5 days of antibiotics** (the course is 10–14 days plus suppression).

**REMEMBER:** *Admit, culture, hydrate, treat for two weeks — then suppress until she delivers.*

`Q11402`

---

## 92. Active Management of the Third Stage

**PEARL:** The WHO components of **ACTIVE MANAGEMENT OF THE THIRD STAGE are: (1) a UTEROTONIC within 1 minute of delivery (OXYTOCIN 10 IU IM is the drug of choice), (2) CONTROLLED CORD TRACTION with counter-traction on the uterus, and (3) assessment of uterine tone.** **SUSTAINED UTERINE MASSAGE IS NO LONGER RECOMMENDED for women who have received a uterotonic**, and **DELAYED cord clamping (1–3 minutes) is now standard — early clamping is NOT part of the package.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The uterotonic is the component that actually prevents haemorrhage — **it reduces PPH by about 60%** — while cord traction merely shortens the third stage. **Delayed clamping was removed from the "early clamping" era because the placental transfusion it allows raises neonatal haemoglobin and iron stores for months and reduces intraventricular haemorrhage and necrotising enterocolitis in preterm babies**, with no increase in maternal bleeding. Evidence changed the bundle; the uterotonic remained.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **EARLY CORD CLAMPING IS INDICATED when the baby needs IMMEDIATE RESUSCITATION (a NON-VIGOROUS/asphyxiated baby), in severe fetomaternal haemorrhage or placental abruption/praevia with maternal haemorrhage, and in monochorionic twins with suspected TTTS.** **Rh-negative mothers, pre-eclampsia and prematurity are NOT indications for early clamping** — preterm babies benefit most from delayed clamping.
- **UMBILICAL CORD MILKING is an alternative when delayed clamping is not possible, but is AVOIDED IN VERY PRETERM (<28 weeks) infants because of intraventricular haemorrhage.**
- **PPH prevention hierarchy: AMTSL for every delivery; TRANEXAMIC ACID within 3 hours for treatment; HEAT-STABLE CARBETOCIN and MISOPROSTOL where refrigeration is unavailable.**
- **The third stage normally lasts 5–15 minutes; RETAINED PLACENTA beyond 30 minutes requires manual removal under anaesthesia with antibiotic cover.**
- **NEVER apply cord traction before the signs of separation, and never without counter-traction — the risk is ACUTE UTERINE INVERSION, which presents with profound shock out of proportion to blood loss and is replaced immediately (JOHNSON'S manoeuvre), with tocolytics if needed and uterotonics only AFTER replacement.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Not included in AMTSL" → **gentle uterine massage** (per the WHO 2012 revision).

**REMEMBER:** *Uterotonic in the first minute, controlled traction, check the tone — and let the cord finish transfusing first.*

`Q11403, Q11417`

---

## 93. Epithelial Ovarian Cancer

**PEARL:** For **advanced epithelial ovarian cancer with peritoneal metastases and ascites, the standard is PRIMARY CYTOREDUCTIVE (DEBULKING) SURGERY FOLLOWED BY PLATINUM–TAXANE CHEMOTHERAPY** (carboplatin + paclitaxel). **NEOADJUVANT chemotherapy followed by interval debulking is reserved for women unfit for primary surgery or with unresectable disease.** **Radiotherapy has no first-line role.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Ovarian cancer spreads **TRANSCOELOMICALLY** — seeding the whole peritoneal cavity — so it is never "localised" in the way other tumours are. The single most powerful prognostic factor after stage is **the volume of RESIDUAL DISEASE: "OPTIMAL" cytoreduction means NO visible residual tumour (R0)**, because chemotherapy penetrates small deposits far better than bulky ones, and bulky masses are poorly vascularised and harbour resistant clones.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **It is the most LETHAL gynaecological cancer because it presents late — vague bloating, early satiety, abdominal discomfort and urinary frequency ("the silent killer"). ~75% present at stage III–IV.**
- **Surgical staging/debulking includes total hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy, OMENTECTOMY, peritoneal washings, multiple biopsies, appendicectomy (for mucinous tumours) and pelvic/para-aortic node assessment.**
- **CA-125 is used for MONITORING RESPONSE, not for diagnosis or population screening (it is raised in endometriosis, fibroids, PID, pregnancy, cirrhosis, tuberculosis and any peritoneal irritation). The RMI (risk of malignancy index) combines CA-125, ultrasound features and menopausal status; HE4 and the ROMA index refine it.**
- **Risk factors: nulliparity, early menarche/late menopause ("incessant ovulation"), family history, and BRCA1/BRCA2 and LYNCH SYNDROME. PROTECTIVE: combined oral contraceptives, pregnancy, breastfeeding and tubal ligation. Risk-reducing SALPINGO-OOPHORECTOMY is offered to BRCA carriers after childbearing.**
- ***PARP INHIBITORS (olaparib, niraparib) as maintenance therapy have transformed outcomes in BRCA-mutated and homologous-recombination-deficient disease*** — so ALL women with non-mucinous epithelial ovarian cancer should be offered GERMLINE AND SOMATIC BRCA TESTING.
- **Most high-grade serous carcinomas are now thought to ORIGINATE IN THE FALLOPIAN TUBE FIMBRIA (serous tubal intraepithelial carcinoma) — hence the interest in opportunistic salpingectomy as prevention.**
- **Germ cell tumours (dysgerminoma, yolk sac) occur in young women, are chemosensitive (BEP), and permit FERTILITY-SPARING unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Advanced disease with ascites → **debulking followed by chemotherapy**.

**REMEMBER:** *Cut out everything you can see, then give platinum — residual disease is the prognosis.*

`Q11405`

---

## 94. The Modified Bishop Score

**PEARL:** The **MODIFIED BISHOP SCORE has FIVE components, each scored 0–3 (0–2 for position and consistency): CERVICAL DILATATION, CERVICAL LENGTH (which replaced effacement), CONSISTENCY, POSITION, and STATION of the presenting part.** A score **≥6–8 is FAVOURABLE for induction; ≤5 warrants CERVICAL RIPENING first.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The score is really a measure of **how far the cervix has already travelled towards labour on its own.** A **posterior, long, firm cervix has not begun the biochemical remodelling** (collagen breakdown, increased hyaluronic acid and water content) that must precede dilatation — so oxytocin will only produce painful contractions against an unyielding cervix, ending in failed induction and caesarean. **Prostaglandins do that remodelling directly**, which is why they are the answer to a low score.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Worked example (score 5): posterior cervix (0) + length 5 cm... note that in the modified scoring, LENGTH 3–4 cm scores 0–1 and progressive shortening scores higher; dilatation 1 cm (1), soft consistency (2), station −1 (2) — read each row carefully, since the paper's arithmetic depends on the exact table used.**
- **Every component moves in the same direction as labour: SOFTER, SHORTER, MORE DILATED, MORE ANTERIOR, LOWER = higher score = more favourable.**
- **RIPENING METHODS for an unfavourable cervix: PGE2 (dinoprostone) gel/pessary, MISOPROSTOL (PGE1 — cheap, heat-stable, effective, but with a higher risk of hyperstimulation; CONTRAINDICATED with a previous caesarean scar), a FOLEY CATHETER BALLOON (mechanical, safest with a scarred uterus), and hygroscopic dilators.**
- **A low Bishop score predicts FAILED INDUCTION and CAESAREAN — particularly in nulliparous women.**
- **MEMBRANE SWEEPING at term reduces the need for formal induction.**
- **After ripening, induce with AMNIOTOMY plus an OXYTOCIN infusion, monitoring contraction frequency (aim ≤5 in 10 minutes) and the fetal heart rate continuously.**
- **UTERINE HYPERSTIMULATION (tachysystole with fetal heart rate changes) is managed by stopping the oxytocin, giving oxygen, left lateral positioning, fluids and a TOCOLYTIC (terbutaline).**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Bishop-score calculations are pure arithmetic — learn the table rows.

**REMEMBER:** *Soft, short, open, anterior and low — the cervix that is already halfway there.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/09/26/untitled-226_ZlB06tA.jpg)

`Q11409`

## 95. High-Dose Folic Acid

**PEARL:** **HIGH-DOSE (4–5 mg/day) periconceptional folic acid is indicated for: a PREVIOUS CHILD WITH A NEURAL TUBE DEFECT (or family history), PRE-GESTATIONAL/POORLY CONTROLLED DIABETES, OBESITY (BMI >30), ANTIEPILEPTIC therapy (valproate, carbamazepine, phenytoin), other FOLATE ANTAGONISTS (methotrexate, trimethoprim, sulfasalazine), HAEMOGLOBINOPATHIES with chronic haemolysis, and malabsorption.** Everyone else takes **400–500 µg.** **Start at least 1–3 MONTHS BEFORE CONCEPTION and continue until 12–16 weeks.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The timing is fixed by embryology: **the neural tube closes by day 28 post-conception — often before the woman knows she is pregnant** — so folate started after a positive pregnancy test is already too late for the primary purpose. Diabetes earns the high dose because **hyperglycaemia is independently teratogenic for the neural tube**, and antiepileptics because they **antagonise folate metabolism directly.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Folic acid supplementation reduces neural tube defect recurrence by ~70% (the MRC Vitamin Study) — one of the most effective preventive interventions in medicine.**
- **Distinguish the other preventive prescriptions in high-risk pregnancy: PREVIOUS PRE-ECLAMPSIA → LOW-DOSE ASPIRIN from 12–16 weeks (plus calcium where intake is low). PREVIOUS PRETERM BIRTH → VAGINAL PROGESTERONE and/or CERCLAGE for a short cervix. SICKLE CELL DISEASE → folic acid (usually 5 mg) plus penicillin prophylaxis, hydration and transfusion planning — but the primary issue is crisis prevention, not neural tube defects.**
- **Routine antenatal supplementation in India: IRON AND FOLIC ACID (100 mg elemental iron + 500 µg folic acid) daily for at least 180 days from the second trimester, plus CALCIUM 1 g/day, and albendazole after the first trimester.**
- **VALPROATE carries the highest teratogenic risk of the antiepileptics (neural tube defects, and impaired neurodevelopment) and should be avoided in women of childbearing potential; LAMOTRIGINE and levetiracetam are preferred.**
- **Excess folate can MASK the haematological signs of vitamin B12 deficiency while the neurological damage progresses — always consider B12 status.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "High-dose folic acid preconceptionally" → **poorly controlled diabetes**.

**REMEMBER:** *The tube closes at four weeks — so folate must be in her before she knows she is pregnant.*

`Q11410`

---

## 96. Ovarian Hyperstimulation Syndrome

**PEARL:** In a woman on **GONADOTROPHINS for infertility, ultrasound showing MULTIPLE ENLARGED FOLLICLES/ENLARGED OVARIES with FREE FLUID = OVARIAN HYPERSTIMULATION SYNDROME.** It is an **IATROGENIC, potentially life-threatening complication, triggered by hCG** (either the ovulation trigger or, worse, the hCG of an ensuing pregnancy).

**UNDERSTAND:** OHSS is a **VEGF-mediated capillary leak syndrome**: luteinised granulosa cells release VEGF under hCG stimulation, causing **massive increases in vascular permeability**. Fluid shifts from the intravascular space into the peritoneum and pleura — producing the paradox that defines the illness: **ASCITES AND OEDEMA WITH INTRAVASCULAR DEPLETION** — hence haemoconcentration, oliguria, hypotension, thrombosis and renal failure, all in a patient who looks fluid-overloaded.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **EARLY OHSS (3–7 days after the trigger) is caused by the exogenous hCG; LATE OHSS (≥12 days) is caused by pregnancy hCG and is more severe and prolonged.**
- **Severity: MILD — abdominal discomfort, ovaries <8 cm. MODERATE — ascites on ultrasound, ovaries 8–12 cm. SEVERE — clinical ascites, ovaries >12 cm, HAEMATOCRIT >45%, WBC >15,000, hypoproteinaemia, oliguria. CRITICAL — tense ascites, pleural effusion/ARDS, thromboembolism, renal failure.**
- **Management: MILD/moderate — outpatient, oral fluids, analgesia (avoid NSAIDs), daily weight and abdominal girth. SEVERE — admit, IV CRYSTALLOIDS then ALBUMIN, THROMBOPROPHYLAXIS WITH LMWH (thrombosis, including unusual sites such as the internal jugular vein, is the major killer), PARACENTESIS for tense ascites, and strict input/output monitoring. DIURETICS ARE CONTRAINDICATED while intravascularly depleted.**
- **Prevention: identify high-risk women (PCOS, young, low BMI, high antral follicle count, high AMH, previous OHSS), use ANTAGONIST protocols with a GnRH AGONIST TRIGGER instead of hCG, "coast" or cancel the cycle, use CABERGOLINE, and adopt a FREEZE-ALL strategy with deferred embryo transfer.**
- **Complications also include OVARIAN TORSION and cyst rupture — the enlarged ovaries are heavy and mobile.**
- **It is SELF-LIMITING if pregnancy does not occur (resolving with menstruation) but PROLONGED if it does.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Enlarged multifollicular ovaries + free fluid + gonadotrophin therapy → **OHSS**.

**REMEMBER:** *VEGF opens the capillaries — she is drowning in the abdomen and dry in the vessels.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/02/picture3.jpg)

`Q11411`

## 97. Radiotherapy Points A and B in Cervical Cancer

**PEARL:** **POINT A is 2 cm SUPERIOR to the external os (along the uterine canal) and 2 cm LATERAL to it — it approximates the crossing of the URETER AND UTERINE ARTERY, and receives ~7000 cGy.** **POINT B is 3 cm LATERAL TO POINT A (i.e. 5 cm from the midline) at the same level — it approximates the PELVIC SIDE WALL/obturator nodes, and receives ~6000 cGy.**

**UNDERSTAND:** These reference points exist because **intracavitary brachytherapy delivers a steeply falling dose**, so the prescription must be anchored to defined anatomical points rather than to the source. **Point A represents the PARACERVICAL TISSUE (the medial parametrium)** — the dose there determines local control and also the dose to the ureter and bladder base, which limits toxicity. **Point B represents the LATERAL PARAMETRIUM AND NODES** — the region treated by external beam.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Treatment of cervical cancer from stage IB3/IIB onwards is CONCURRENT CHEMORADIATION: external beam radiotherapy plus WEEKLY CISPLATIN (a radiosensitiser) plus INTRACAVITARY BRACHYTHERAPY (tandem and ovoids). Brachytherapy is ESSENTIAL — omitting it worsens survival.**
- **Modern practice has moved to IMAGE-GUIDED (MRI-based) VOLUMETRIC planning with high-risk CTV doses, but points A and B remain the classical examinable landmarks.**
- **Acute radiation toxicity: diarrhoea, cystitis, proctitis, marrow suppression, skin reactions. LATE toxicity: vaginal stenosis and dryness, radiation proctitis and cystitis with bleeding, fistula formation, small bowel obstruction, insufficiency fractures, and OVARIAN FAILURE — hence ovarian TRANSPOSITION in young women.**
- **Radioisotopes used in brachytherapy: historically RADIUM-226, then CAESIUM-137; modern high-dose-rate units use IRIDIUM-192 (with COBALT-60 for external beam).**
- **The MANCHESTER SYSTEM (points A and B) and the classical Stockholm and Paris systems are the historical brachytherapy schemes.**
- **Surgery is for early disease (up to IB2/IIA1); radiotherapy can treat ALL stages; and the two are not combined electively because the morbidity of surgery plus radiation is high.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "False statement" → **point A is 5 cm lateral** (it is 2 cm lateral, 2 cm superior).

**REMEMBER:** *Two up and two out is point A at the ureter; three further out is point B at the pelvic wall.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/08/12/screenshot-2024-08-12-104746.png)
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/08/12/screenshot-2024-08-12-104945.png)

`Q11412, Q11413`

## 98. Azoospermia — Obstructive versus Non-Obstructive

**PEARL:** **TESTICULAR BIOPSY distinguishes OBSTRUCTIVE from NON-OBSTRUCTIVE azoospermia** — showing **NORMAL spermatogenesis in obstruction** and **absent or severely reduced spermatogenesis in testicular failure.** **FSH and testicular volume give the initial clue: NORMAL FSH with NORMAL-sized testes suggests OBSTRUCTION; HIGH FSH with SMALL, soft testes indicates PRIMARY TESTICULAR FAILURE; LOW FSH and LH indicate a PRE-TESTICULAR (hypogonadotrophic) cause.**

**UNDERSTAND:** FSH is regulated by **INHIBIN B from Sertoli cells**, so it is effectively **a blood test of seminiferous tubule function**: when the germinal epithelium is destroyed, inhibin falls and FSH rises. Obstruction leaves the tubules intact, so **FSH stays normal**. Biopsy is confirmatory because FSH is only a proxy — and biopsy has the added advantage that **sperm retrieved at the same time can be used for ICSI.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Causes: OBSTRUCTIVE — congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens (associated with CFTR/CYSTIC FIBROSIS mutations — screen the partner), vasectomy, epididymal obstruction after infection (tuberculosis, gonorrhoea, chlamydia), and ejaculatory duct obstruction. NON-OBSTRUCTIVE — KLINEFELTER SYNDROME (47,XXY), Y-chromosome AZF microdeletions, cryptorchidism, mumps orchitis, chemotherapy/radiotherapy, varicocele, and heat. PRE-TESTICULAR — hypogonadotrophic hypogonadism (Kallmann's), hyperprolactinaemia, and exogenous ANABOLIC STEROIDS (a common and reversible cause).**
- **Semen VOLUME matters: a LOW-VOLUME, acidic, fructose-negative ejaculate suggests EJACULATORY DUCT OBSTRUCTION or absent vas; a normal volume suggests obstruction higher up or testicular failure. POST-EJACULATORY URINALYSIS detects RETROGRADE EJACULATION (diabetes, bladder neck surgery, alpha-blockers).**
- **Investigations: repeat semen analysis (centrifuged), FSH/LH/testosterone/prolactin, KARYOTYPE and Y-microdeletion testing (mandatory before ICSI, since the defect is transmitted to sons), CFTR testing when the vas is absent, and scrotal/transrectal ultrasound.**
- **Treatment: OBSTRUCTIVE — surgical reconstruction or sperm retrieval (PESA/MESA) with ICSI (excellent results). NON-OBSTRUCTIVE — micro-TESE with ICSI (sperm found in ~50%); donor sperm if none.**
- **WHO 2021 reference values: volume ≥1.4 mL, concentration ≥16 million/mL, total motility ≥42%, PROGRESSIVE motility ≥30% (the older WHO 2010 value was 32%), normal morphology ≥4%.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Differentiate obstructive from testicular cause" → **testicular biopsy**.

**REMEMBER:** *FSH is a blood test of the tubules; the biopsy settles it and may find sperm at the same time.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/06/07/screenshot-2024-06-07-123823.png)

`Q11416, Q11421`

## 99. Innervation of the Uterus and Labour Pain

**PEARL:** **SENSORY fibres from the uterus and cervix run with the sympathetics to T10–L1** — so **FIRST-STAGE labour pain (uterine contraction and cervical dilatation) is referred to the T10–L1 dermatomes (lower abdomen, back)**. **SECOND-STAGE pain (perineal distension) travels in the PUDENDAL NERVE, S2–S4.** **UTERINE CONTRACTILITY IS NOT NEURALLY DRIVEN — it is HORMONAL/myogenic** (oestrogen, prostaglandins and oxytocin; progesterone maintains quiescence).

**UNDERSTAND:** The proof that contraction is not neurogenic is clinical: **a woman with a complete spinal cord transection above T10 still labours normally and delivers**, feeling nothing. The uterus has an intrinsic pacemaker in the fundal myometrium with gap-junction (connexin-43) coupling that increases at term. This is exactly why **regional anaesthesia abolishes the PAIN without abolishing LABOUR** — and why an epidural is safe.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Anaesthetic consequences: an EPIDURAL for the first stage must cover T10–L1; for the second stage it must extend to S2–S4. For CAESAREAN SECTION the block must reach T4 (peritoneal traction), which is why hypotension is common and why phenylephrine and left lateral tilt are used.**
- **A PUDENDAL BLOCK (injected transvaginally at the ISCHIAL SPINE) anaesthetises the perineum for outlet forceps, episiotomy and repair — but does NOT relieve uterine pain.**
- **PARACERVICAL BLOCK relieves first-stage pain but risks FETAL BRADYCARDIA and is little used.**
- **Epidural analgesia: prolongs the second stage and increases instrumental delivery, but does NOT increase caesarean rates; it may cause hypotension, fever, urinary retention and POST-DURAL PUNCTURE HEADACHE.**
- **The autonomic supply reaches the uterus via the INFERIOR HYPOGASTRIC (pelvic) PLEXUS and the uterovaginal (FRANKENHÄUSER'S) plexus. Sympathetic T5–L2; parasympathetic S2–S4.**
- **In LABOUR, pain in early first stage is VISCERAL and poorly localised; it becomes SOMATIC, sharp and well-localised as the presenting part distends the pelvic floor and perineum.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Incorrect regarding uterine innervation" → **contractility is mediated by T7–T8 innervation** (it is hormonal).

**REMEMBER:** *T10 to L1 for the contractions, S2 to S4 for the perineum — and the uterus contracts without any nerves at all.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/09/27/untitled-242.jpg)

`Q11422`

## 100. Reading the CTG — Decelerations

**PEARL:** **LATE DECELERATIONS are SMOOTH, GRADUAL, symmetrical falls in the fetal heart rate that BEGIN AFTER THE PEAK of the contraction and RETURN TO BASELINE AFTER the contraction ends — indicating UTEROPLACENTAL INSUFFICIENCY.** They are defined by **TIMING, not by an arbitrary count of beats** (a fall of ≥15 bpm lasting ≥15 seconds is the usual quantification, but the "10 beats twice in 20 minutes above baseline" description belongs to ACCELERATIONS, not decelerations).

**UNDERSTAND:** The delay is the diagnosis. **Uterine contraction transiently reduces intervillous perfusion; a healthy fetus tolerates it, but a compromised one becomes hypoxaemic, and the chemoreceptor-mediated response takes time to develop** — hence the deceleration lags behind the contraction. **EARLY decelerations mirror the contraction exactly** because they are a vagal reflex to head compression (benign). **VARIABLE decelerations are abrupt and variable in shape and timing** because they follow cord compression.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The DR C BRAVADO framework: Define Risk, Contractions, Baseline RAte, VAriability, Decelerations, Overall impression.**
- **Normal CTG: baseline 110–160 bpm, variability 5–25 bpm, accelerations present, no decelerations. ACCELERATIONS: ≥15 bpm for ≥15 seconds (≥10 bpm for ≥10 seconds before 32 weeks).**
- **REDUCED VARIABILITY (<5 bpm for >50 minutes) is the most ominous single feature after a prolonged bradycardia — causes include fetal sleep (up to 40 minutes), hypoxia/acidosis, prematurity, drugs (opioids, magnesium, sedatives), and fetal anomaly.**
- **A SINUSOIDAL pattern — a smooth regular sine wave with absent variability — indicates SEVERE FETAL ANAEMIA (fetomaternal haemorrhage, Rh disease, vasa praevia) and is an emergency.**
- **Management of a pathological trace: change to the LEFT LATERAL position, give oxygen and IV fluids, STOP OXYTOCIN, exclude cord prolapse and hypotension, consider tocolysis; then FETAL SCALP BLOOD SAMPLING where available (pH <7.20 = deliver) or expedite delivery.**
- **RECURRENT LATE DECELERATIONS WITH ABSENT VARIABILITY is category III — deliver.** **EARLY decelerations require no action.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "False regarding late decelerations" → the **"10 beats twice in 20 minutes above baseline"** description (that defines accelerations).

**REMEMBER:** *Early mirrors the contraction, variable ignores it, late lags behind it — and only late means the placenta is failing.*

`Q11428`

---

## 101. Classification of Forceps Delivery

**PEARL:** **OUTLET FORCEPS: the scalp is VISIBLE AT THE INTROITUS WITHOUT SEPARATING THE LABIA, the skull has reached the pelvic floor, the head is ON THE PERINEUM, and rotation does not exceed 45°.** **LOW forceps: leading point of the skull at STATION ≥ +2 but not on the pelvic floor (subdivided by rotation ≤45° or >45°).** **MID forceps: head ENGAGED but the station is above +2.** **HIGH forceps (unengaged head) IS OBSOLETE AND NEVER PERFORMED.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The classification is a **risk ladder**: the higher the station and the greater the rotation required, the more force is transmitted and the greater the maternal and fetal trauma. That is why **outlet forceps is a safe, atraumatic assist while mid-cavity forceps demands an experienced operator, theatre availability and a prepared caesarean back-up ("trial of instrumental delivery in theatre")**, and why high forceps was abandoned entirely once safe caesarean became available.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **ENGAGEMENT is essential before any instrument: the biparietal diameter has passed the pelvic brim, i.e. no more than 2/5 of the head is palpable abdominally. NEVER apply forceps or ventouse to an unengaged head.**
- **The prerequisites (mnemonic "ABCDEFGHIJ"): Address the patient/consent, Bladder empty, Cervix FULLY dilated, Determine position and station, Equipment ready, Fontanelles identified, Gentle traction, Handle elevation, Incision (episiotomy) as needed, remove Jaws (blades) and reassess.**
- **Deep transverse arrest in a multipara at station 0 with MOULDING 2+ and CAPUT 2+ is a picture of CEPHALOPELVIC DISPROPORTION — the correct management is CAESAREAN SECTION, not mid-cavity forceps or ventouse. Caput and moulding are the warning signs that a vaginal attempt will fail.**
- **Forceps functions: TRACTION, ROTATION and COMPRESSION of the head (and protection of the preterm head at caesarean).**
- **Complications: maternal — third/fourth-degree tears, cervical and vaginal lacerations, PPH, urinary retention; fetal — facial bruising and FACIAL NERVE PALSY, skull fracture, cephalhaematoma, and rarely intracranial haemorrhage.**
- **Ventouse and forceps must NEVER be used sequentially — neonatal morbidity rises steeply.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Outlet forceps applied at" → **head on the perineum**.

**REMEMBER:** *If you can see the scalp without parting the labia, it is outlet — and if the head is not engaged, no instrument may be used at all.*

## 102. Anti-D After Delivery — Reading the Baby's Blood

**PEARL:** After delivery, give **ANTI-D to an Rh-NEGATIVE mother when the BABY IS Rh-POSITIVE AND THE DIRECT COOMBS TEST (DCT) IS NEGATIVE.** **If the baby is Rh-NEGATIVE, no anti-D is needed** (there is nothing to sensitise against). **If the DCT is POSITIVE, the mother is ALREADY SENSITISED and anti-D is futile** — her own antibodies are already coating the baby's cells.

**UNDERSTAND:** The DCT detects **maternal antibody already bound to fetal red cells**. A positive DCT therefore means **the immune response has already happened** — prophylaxis, which works by clearing fetal cells before the maternal immune system recognises them, can no longer achieve anything. This is precisely the same logic that excludes anti-D after an intrauterine transfusion.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Give 300 µg (1500 IU) IM WITHIN 72 HOURS of delivery; it is still worth giving up to 10–13 days if missed. Routine antenatal prophylaxis is 300 µg at 28 weeks (± 34 weeks).**
- **Perform a KLEIHAUER–BETKE TEST (or flow cytometry) after delivery — and after abruption, trauma, manual removal of placenta, external cephalic version or a twin delivery — to quantify FETOMATERNAL HAEMORRHAGE and give ADDITIONAL doses if it exceeds the 30 mL of fetal whole blood covered by one dose.**
- **Anti-D is also indicated for: any abortion (spontaneous or induced), ectopic and molar pregnancy, fetal death at any gestation, invasive procedures (amniocentesis, CVS, cordocentesis), external cephalic version, abdominal trauma, and antepartum haemorrhage.**
- **A weakly positive DCT can also follow ANTENATAL anti-D prophylaxis itself (passively acquired antibody) — this does NOT mean sensitisation, and postnatal anti-D should still be given if the baby is Rh-positive.**
- **In a SENSITISED pregnancy, management shifts from prevention to surveillance: maternal antibody TITRES, then MCA PEAK SYSTOLIC VELOCITY >1.5 MoM → cordocentesis and INTRAUTERINE TRANSFUSION.**
- **ABO incompatibility between mother and fetus partially protects against Rh sensitisation.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Read the two variables — **baby's Rh status and the DCT**. Positive baby + negative DCT = give it.

**REMEMBER:** *Anti-D only helps a mother who has not yet reacted — a positive Coombs means you are too late.*

`Q11424`

## 103. Office Endometrial Sampling

**PEARL:** The **PIPELLE (a thin flexible plastic suction cannula) is used for OFFICE ENDOMETRIAL SAMPLING** — it needs **NO cervical dilatation and NO anaesthesia**, is nearly painless, and has ~90% accuracy for endometrial carcinoma. Its limitation is that it is a **BLIND procedure — focal lesions (polyps, focal hyperplasia, submucous fibroids) can be MISSED**, so persistent bleeding after a negative Pipelle demands **HYSTEROSCOPY.**

**UNDERSTAND:** A Pipelle samples only a **fraction of the cavity's surface**, which is why it performs excellently for **DIFFUSE processes (endometrial carcinoma is usually global by the time it bleeds)** and poorly for **focal ones**. That single distinction dictates the algorithm: a **positive result is trustworthy; a NEGATIVE result in a woman who keeps bleeding is not.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Match the instrument to the sample: AYRE'S SPATULA — ectocervix/squamocolumnar junction for a Pap smear; CYTOBRUSH — endocervical canal; PIPELLE/Sharman curette — endometrium; HEGAR'S DILATORS then a curette — D&C; VOLSELLUM/tenaculum — to steady the cervix; UTERINE SOUND — to measure cavity length and direction.**
- **FRACTIONAL CURETTAGE samples the ENDOCERVIX FIRST and then the ENDOMETRIUM SEPARATELY — it tells you whether an endometrial carcinoma has involved the cervix, which changes the stage and the surgery.**
- **HYSTEROSCOPY is the gold standard for the cavity because it is a DIRECT-VISION, TARGETED biopsy — indicated for a negative or insufficient blind sample with continued bleeding, for suspected polyps or submucous fibroids, and for Asherman's syndrome.**
- **Indications for endometrial sampling: POSTMENOPAUSAL BLEEDING (mandatory), abnormal uterine bleeding over age 40–45, younger women with risk factors (obesity, PCOS, anovulation, tamoxifen, Lynch syndrome), endometrial thickness >4 mm after the menopause, atypical glandular cells on a Pap smear, and infertility work-up for tuberculous endometritis (a PREMENSTRUAL sample).**
- **Contraindications: pregnancy and acute pelvic infection.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Image of a thin flexible cannula → **endometrial sampling**.

**REMEMBER:** *A Pipelle that finds cancer is right; a Pipelle that finds nothing in a woman still bleeding proves nothing.*

![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/07/19/untitled-1038.jpg)
