## 138. Cervical Cancer FIGO Staging — Hydronephrosis is IIIB

**PEARL:** **Hydronephrosis or a non-functioning kidney = FIGO stage IIIB**, regardless of
tumour size. **Positive para-aortic nodes = IIIC2** (pelvic nodes = IIIC1) — but
hydronephrosis in this stem still fixes the stage at **IIIB** as the assigned answer, and
nodal disease is **not** M1.

**UNDERSTAND:** Hydronephrosis is taken as **proof of pelvic sidewall involvement** — the
ureter is compressed as it crosses the pelvic brim — so the staging system upgrades on
that functional finding alone, without needing to demonstrate the tumour touching the
wall. This is a rare instance of a **physiological consequence defining a stage**.

**MUST KNOW (FIGO 2018/2021):**
- **IA** microscopic (IA1 ≤3 mm depth, IA2 >3–5 mm); **IB1** ≤2 cm, **IB2** 2–4 cm, **IB3** >4 cm.
- **II** beyond uterus, not to pelvic wall/lower third of vagina: **IIA** upper vagina (IIA1 ≤4 cm, IIA2 >4 cm), **IIB parametrium**.
- **III: IIIA** lower third of vagina; **IIIB pelvic sidewall or HYDRONEPHROSIS/non-functioning kidney**; **IIIC1 pelvic nodes, IIIC2 para-aortic nodes** (add "r" for radiology, "p" for pathology).
- **IVA** bladder/rectal mucosa (must be biopsy-proven — bullous oedema alone is not enough); **IVB** distant metastasis.
- Staging is **clinical**, now permitted to use imaging and pathology. **Parametrial involvement (IIB) is the cut-off for surgery**: IA–IB2/IIA1 → radical hysterectomy (Wertheim); **IIB and above → concurrent chemoradiation with cisplatin**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Any cervical cancer stem mentioning **hydronephrosis** answers itself
— **IIIB**. The distractor is upgrading to IVB because of "positive nodes on PET".

**REMEMBER:** *A blocked ureter means the sidewall is involved — that's IIIB.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/12/18/picture23_ZC18iSO.jpg)

`Q9598`

## 139. Normal Menstrual Parameters (FIGO 2018) and PCOS Criteria

**PEARL:** FIGO's revised normal **cycle frequency is 24–38 days** (not the old 21–35),
duration ≤8 days, and volume "not excessive by the patient's judgement".

**UNDERSTAND:** FIGO deliberately replaced Latin terms (menorrhagia, metrorrhagia,
oligomenorrhoea) with plain descriptors of **frequency, regularity, duration and volume**,
because the old terms were used inconsistently. The numbers were narrowed to reflect the
5th–95th centiles of real populations.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Frequency:** frequent <24 days, normal **24–38**, infrequent >38. **Regularity:** variation ≤7–9 days. **Duration:** normal ≤8 days. **Volume:** heavy menstrual bleeding = interference with quality of life.
- **PCOS — Rotterdam criteria, 2 of 3:** (1) **oligo/anovulation**, (2) **clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism**, (3) **polycystic ovarian morphology on USS** (≥12 [now ≥20 with modern probes] follicles of 2–9 mm in **one or both** ovaries, or ovarian volume >10 mL). Other causes (thyroid, prolactin, CAH, Cushing) must be excluded.
- PCOS features that are TRUE: **testosterone >2 ng/mL** (values above 200 ng/dL suggest an androgen-secreting tumour), **infertility**, **polycystic morphology in one OR both ovaries**.
- Features that are FALSE and appear as distractors: "**high FSH/LH ratio**" — it is the **LH:FSH ratio that is raised (≥2–3:1)**; and "**fasting glucose/insulin ratio >4.5**" — a ratio **<4.5 indicates insulin resistance**.
- Management: **weight loss** first-line; **letrozole** is now preferred over clomiphene for ovulation induction; combined OCP for cycle control and hirsutism; **metformin** for metabolic features; long-term risk of **endometrial hyperplasia and carcinoma** from unopposed oestrogen.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The multi-statement PCOS question hinges on spotting the two
**inverted ratios** (LH:FSH, glucose:insulin).

**REMEMBER:** *24 to 38 is normal now; LH is the high one and the glucose:insulin ratio is
the low one.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/06/15/untitled-761.jpg)

`Q9597, Q9586`

## 140. Obstetric Conjugates and Pelvic Assessment

**PEARL:** The **diagonal conjugate is the only conjugate that can be MEASURED clinically**
— from the **lower border of the symphysis pubis to the sacral promontory (~12.5 cm)**.
The **obstetric conjugate — the shortest and functionally critical anteroposterior
diameter of the inlet (~10 cm) — is derived by subtracting 1.5–2 cm from it.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The obstetric conjugate runs from the **inner, most prominent point on
the back of the symphysis** to the promontory — a point no examining finger can reach.
Hence the clinical work-around: measure what you can reach (diagonal) and subtract the
thickness and inclination of the symphysis.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **True (anatomical) conjugate:** upper border of symphysis → promontory, **11 cm**.
- **Obstetric conjugate:** ~**10 cm**, **the shortest AP diameter of the inlet** and the one the head must negotiate.
- **Diagonal conjugate:** **12.5 cm**; if the promontory cannot be reached on examination, the inlet is presumed adequate.
- **Inlet:** transverse **13 cm** (widest), oblique 12 cm — so the head engages in the **transverse**. **Outlet:** AP **13 cm** (widest), **intertuberous 11 cm** — so the head is delivered in the **AP** diameter; this is why **internal rotation** occurs.
- **Gynaecoid** pelvis is the ideal (round inlet); **android** (heart-shaped, narrow forepelvis, prominent spines) has the worst outcome for vaginal delivery; **anthropoid** favours occipito-posterior; **platypelloid** favours transverse arrest.
- Clinical pelvimetry has poor predictive value; **X-ray pelvimetry is obsolete** — trial of labour is the true test.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Image-based ("which dimension is being measured?" — fingers reaching
the promontory = **diagonal conjugate**) and the numeric recall of which conjugate is
shortest (**obstetric**).

**REMEMBER:** *Feel the diagonal, subtract two, and you have the obstetric conjugate.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/06/13/obg-13.jpg)
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/01/13/screenshot-2024-01-13-174838.jpg)

`Q9596`

## 141. Asymptomatic Bacteriuria in Pregnancy

**PEARL:** **Untreated asymptomatic bacteriuria progresses to symptomatic infection in
about 25%**, and to **pyelonephritis in 20–30%** — which is why **every pregnant woman is
screened with a urine culture at the first antenatal visit** and treated even without
symptoms.

**UNDERSTAND:** Pregnancy converts a harmless colonisation into a dangerous one.
**Progesterone causes ureteric smooth-muscle relaxation and the gravid uterus compresses
the ureters (right more than left)**, producing physiological hydronephrosis, urinary
stasis and vesicoureteric reflux — a perfect ascending route. Glycosuria adds substrate.
That is why ASB, which needs no treatment outside pregnancy, must be treated in it.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Definition: **≥10⁵ CFU/mL of a single organism on a clean-catch midstream specimen, without symptoms**. Prevalence 2–7%. Commonest organism **E. coli**; also *Klebsiella*, *Proteus*, **Group B Streptococcus** (whose presence in urine mandates intrapartum GBS prophylaxis regardless of the later swab).
- Untreated ASB is associated with **pyelonephritis, preterm labour, low birth weight and perinatal mortality**; treatment prevents most of these.
- Safe antibiotics: **nitrofurantoin** (avoid at term/G6PD — haemolysis), **cephalexin**, **amoxicillin/co-amoxiclav**, **fosfomycin** single dose. **Avoid: trimethoprim in the first trimester** (antifolate), **fluoroquinolones** (cartilage), **tetracyclines** (teeth/bone), **sulfonamides near term** (kernicterus), aminoglycosides (ototoxicity).
- Treat for **3–7 days**, then **repeat culture as test of cure**; recurrent episodes → suppressive prophylaxis for the rest of the pregnancy.
- **Acute pyelonephritis in pregnancy** = admit, IV antibiotics, IV fluids — it is a leading non-obstetric cause of **ARDS and septic shock** in pregnancy.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Numeric recall (25% become symptomatic, 20–30% pyelonephritis) or a
"which antibiotic is safe" question.

**REMEMBER:** *In pregnancy the ureters are lazy — silent bacteria climb, so always treat.*

`Q9581`

---

## 142. Physiological Cardiovascular Changes of Pregnancy

**PEARL:** **Lateral displacement of the apex beat** is normal in pregnancy — the
diaphragm rises. A **soft systolic flow murmur** and the continuous **mammary souffle**
are also physiological; **any diastolic murmur, pansystolic murmur or bradycardia is
pathological.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Pregnancy is a **high-output, low-resistance** state: plasma volume rises
~50%, cardiac output 30–50%, heart rate by 10–20 bpm, while systemic vascular resistance
falls. Increased flow across normal valves and reduced blood viscosity generate an
**ejection systolic murmur**; diastolic filling murmurs have no physiological explanation,
so they always indicate disease (classically **mitral stenosis**, which decompensates in
pregnancy).

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Rises:** plasma volume (peaks 32 weeks), cardiac output (peaks ~25–30 weeks, then again immediately postpartum by up to 80% from autotransfusion), heart rate, stroke volume.
- **Falls:** SVR, BP (nadir in the **second trimester**, back to baseline by term), haematocrit (**physiological anaemia of pregnancy** — plasma rises more than red cell mass), colloid oncotic pressure.
- **Unchanged/normal signs:** loud split S1, **S3 gallop**, ejection systolic murmur at the left sternal edge, apex displaced **up and left**, larger cardiac silhouette, left-axis deviation on ECG, small Q and inverted T in lead III.
- **Aortocaval (supine hypotensive) syndrome** after 20 weeks — nurse in the **left lateral tilt**.
- The most dangerous times for a woman with cardiac disease are **28–32 weeks (peak volume), labour, and the immediate postpartum period**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which cardiovascular change would you EXPECT?" — pick the one that
follows from a raised diaphragm and increased flow. All diastolic murmurs are wrong.

**REMEMBER:** *Pregnancy makes the heart flow faster, not fill worse — systolic murmurs
are fine, diastolic never.*

`Q10830`

---

## 143. Acute Uterine Inversion

**PEARL:** Profuse postpartum haemorrhage with a **mass protruding through the introitus
and no palpable fundus abdominally**, after **cord traction before placental separation**,
is **acute uterine inversion**. Management: **resuscitate, attempt immediate manual
repositioning (Johnson manoeuvre), and give TOCOLYTICS if the ring has clamped down —
and do NOT remove the placenta until the uterus is replaced.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Two rules explain the entire management. First, the placenta is
temporarily **plugging the bleeding surface**, so detaching it before replacement converts
a controllable situation into torrential haemorrhage. Second, once the cervical/uterine
ring contracts around the inverted fundus, replacement becomes mechanically impossible —
which is why you need **relaxation (tocolysis)**, not more force. Only after the uterus is
back inside do you reverse the logic and give **oxytocics** to keep it there.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Sequence: **call for help → IV access, fluids, blood, treat shock** (which is out of proportion to blood loss — vagal/neurogenic from traction on the infundibulopelvic ligaments) → **immediate manual replacement** (push the part that inverted last, back first) → if it fails, **tocolysis (terbutaline, MgSO₄, GTN) or general anaesthesia with halothane** → **hydrostatic reduction (O'Sullivan)** → surgery (**Huntington** — traction from above; **Haultain** — posterior ring incision) as the last resort.
- **Give oxytocin/ergometrine only AFTER replacement**, then keep a hand inside until the uterus contracts.
- Risk factors: **fundal placenta**, uterine atony (**polyhydramnios**, multiple pregnancy, prolonged labour), **cord traction with an uncontracted uterus**, morbidly adherent placenta, short cord, connective-tissue disorders.
- **Degrees:** 1st — fundus dimples into the cavity; 2nd — fundus through the cervix into the vagina; 3rd — fundus through the **introitus**; 4th (total) — vagina inverted as well.
- **Prevention: active management of the third stage** — oxytocin, **controlled cord traction with counter-pressure on the uterus (Brandt–Andrews)**, never traction on an uncontracted uterus.
- A **Bakri balloon** treats atony with an intact uterus; it has no role in inversion until the uterus is replaced.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem always contains premature cord traction plus shock out of
proportion to blood loss. The discriminating answer includes **tocolysis** and leaves the
placenta alone.

**REMEMBER:** *Replace first, detach second, contract third.*

`Q10845`

---

## 144. Anembryonic Pregnancy and Definitive Ultrasound Criteria

**PEARL:** A **mean sac diameter ≥25 mm with no embryo** diagnoses an **anembryonic
pregnancy (blighted ovum) with certainty**. The parallel rule: **crown–rump length ≥7 mm
with no cardiac activity = missed abortion.**

**UNDERSTAND:** These thresholds were deliberately raised (from 16 mm and 5 mm) to give
**100% specificity — zero false positives**. Terminating a wanted, viable pregnancy on a
false diagnosis is unacceptable, so the criteria are set to sacrifice sensitivity for
certainty. When a scan is below these cut-offs, the correct answer is never "diagnose
failure" but **"repeat the scan in 7–14 days"**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Definitive of pregnancy failure:** CRL ≥7 mm without heartbeat; **MSD ≥25 mm without an embryo**; absence of an embryo with heartbeat **≥2 weeks after a scan showing a sac without a yolk sac**, or **≥11 days after a scan showing a sac with a yolk sac**.
- **Suspicious but NOT diagnostic** (repeat the scan): CRL <7 mm without heartbeat, MSD 16–24 mm without embryo, empty amnion, enlarged yolk sac (>7 mm), small sac relative to embryo (<5 mm difference).
- Normal milestones: **gestational sac 4.5–5 weeks, yolk sac 5.5 weeks, embryo with cardiac activity 6 weeks**; **β-hCG discriminatory zone 1500–2000 mIU/mL** for TVS.
- Management of early pregnancy loss: **expectant** (up to 2 weeks), **medical (misoprostol ± mifepristone)**, or **surgical (manual vacuum aspiration)** — offer choice unless there is haemorrhage, sepsis or instability, when surgical evacuation is indicated.
- **Anti-D** for Rh-negative women after any surgical evacuation or significant bleeding.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The single number is the question — **25 mm** for the sac, **7 mm**
for the CRL. Both older values (16 mm, 5 mm) will be offered as distractors.

**REMEMBER:** *Seven for the embryo, twenty-five for the sac — and when in doubt, scan again.*

`Q10849`

---

## 145. Emergency Contraception: The 5-Day Method

**PEARL:** The **copper IUCD is the only emergency contraceptive effective up to 5 days
(120 hours)** after unprotected intercourse — every hormonal method must be given within
**72 hours**. It is also the **most effective** (failure <1%).

**UNDERSTAND:** Hormonal methods work by **delaying or preventing ovulation**, so once
ovulation has already occurred they are useless — which caps their window at ~72 hours
after intercourse. The copper IUCD works **post-fertilisation**, by a **direct
spermicidal effect of copper ions and by preventing implantation** through a sterile
inflammatory endometrial reaction — so it still works after ovulation, right up to the
point of implantation at day 5–7.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Levonorgestrel 1.5 mg** — within **72 hours** (efficacy falls with time and with **BMI >26**); the standard OTC method.
- **Ulipristal acetate 30 mg** — a selective progesterone receptor modulator, effective up to **120 hours** and superior to levonorgestrel close to ovulation (the one hormonal exception to the 72-hour rule).
- **Yuzpe regimen** (combined OCP, 2 doses 12 hours apart) — within 72 hours; largely obsolete because of vomiting.
- **Centchroman (ormeloxifene)** — an Indian SERM: 2 × 30 mg tablets 12 hours apart within 72 hours for emergency use; as a regular contraceptive it is taken **twice weekly for 12 weeks then weekly** (Saheli).
- **Mifepristone** low dose is also effective within 72 hours.
- **Copper IUCD** — insert within **5 days of intercourse, or within 5 days of the earliest expected ovulation**; the bonus is that it then provides **10 years of ongoing contraception**. Avoid in suspected infection, PID, or an existing pregnancy.
- Emergency contraception is **not abortifacient** in the legal sense — none of these methods disrupts an established (implanted) pregnancy.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem gives a time interval. **>72 hours → copper IUCD** (or
ulipristal if offered); ≤72 hours → levonorgestrel.

**REMEMBER:** *Pills stop ovulation and expire at 72 hours; the copper coil stops
implantation and lasts to day 5.*

`Q10840`

---

## 146. Meigs Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Meigs syndrome = ovarian FIBROMA + ascites + pleural effusion (classically
RIGHT-sided)**, all of which **resolve completely after removing the tumour**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The fibroma is a benign, solid, oedematous tumour that **transudates fluid
from its surface** into the peritoneum; the fluid then tracks into the chest through
**diaphragmatic lymphatics and small congenital fenestrations, which are more numerous on
the right**. Nothing is malignant and nothing has metastasised — which is why removal is
curative and why the syndrome is a classic **benign mimic of ovarian carcinoma**
(especially since **CA-125 can be raised**).

**MUST KNOW:**
- **True Meigs:** fibroma (also accepted: **thecoma, granulosa cell tumour, Brenner tumour**) + ascites + hydrothorax + **resolution after resection**.
- **Pseudo-Meigs:** the same triad with **other tumours** — mature teratoma, **struma ovarii**, mucinous cystadenoma, uterine leiomyoma, metastatic disease.
- **Pseudo-pseudo Meigs (Tjalma syndrome):** ascites + pleural effusion + raised CA-125 in **SLE**, with **no tumour at all**.
- **Ovarian fibroma:** the commonest benign solid ovarian tumour; **oestrogen-inactive**; firm, white, whorled cut surface, spindle cells; associated with **Gorlin (basal cell naevus) syndrome** when bilateral and calcified.
- Contrast the functioning sex cord–stromal tumours: **granulosa cell tumour** — oestrogen → precocious puberty or postmenopausal bleeding, **Call–Exner bodies**, **inhibin** as tumour marker; **Sertoli–Leydig** — androgen → **virilisation**, Reinke crystals.
- The presence of ascites and a pleural effusion with an ovarian mass does **not** establish malignancy — the diagnosis is made after surgery.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The named triad is asked as "which tumour" (**fibroma**), or the
distinction Meigs vs pseudo-Meigs is tested by substituting struma ovarii/teratoma.

**REMEMBER:** *Benign fibroma, right-sided effusion, cured by the knife.*

`Q10848`

---

## 147. Yolk Sac Tumour (Endodermal Sinus Tumour)

**PEARL:** **Premenarchal girl + rapidly growing ovarian mass + raised AFP + Schiller–Duval
bodies** (a central capillary surrounded by tumour cells within a cystic space —
"glomeruloid") = **yolk sac tumour**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The tumour recapitulates the **extraembryonic yolk sac and its endodermal
sinus**, which is precisely the structure that secretes **AFP** in the fetus — so the
histology and the tumour marker are the same fact seen two ways. Schiller–Duval bodies are
an attempted reconstruction of the primitive glomerulus-like endodermal sinus.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Second commonest malignant ovarian germ cell tumour after dysgerminoma; peak in the **second decade**; **aggressive, unilateral, grows fast**, presents with abdominal pain from torsion/rupture.
- Treatment: **fertility-sparing unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy + surgical staging, followed by BEP chemotherapy (bleomycin, etoposide, cisplatin)** — highly chemosensitive, prognosis now good.
- **Germ cell tumour markers:** **AFP → yolk sac tumour** (and mixed tumours with a yolk sac element, hepatoid tumours); **β-hCG → choriocarcinoma** (and syncytiotrophoblastic giant cells in dysgerminoma); **LDH → dysgerminoma**; **AFP and hCG both normal → mature teratoma**.
- **Dysgerminoma:** the ovarian counterpart of seminoma — sheets of clear, glycogen-rich cells separated by fibrous septa **infiltrated by lymphocytes**; the **only** ovarian germ cell tumour that is **commonly bilateral (10–15%)** and the most **radiosensitive**; associated with **gonadal dysgenesis/Turner with Y material**.
- **Krukenberg tumour:** bilateral ovarian metastases of **signet-ring cells**, classically from **gastric** carcinoma — a metastasis, never a primary.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Any ovarian mass in a child or teenager with a marker — match marker
to histology. **Schiller–Duval = AFP = yolk sac** is the single highest-yield triple.

**REMEMBER:** *The yolk sac made AFP in the fetus, and its tumour still does.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://cdn1.dailyrounds.org/uploads/bd2b5020f67f4f4caf02bb8405d8b376.JPEG)
![explanation](https://dhmbxeygs57ff.cloudfront.net/uploads/658ad21b17c54c6f8e16bd12131fbb43x800x600.JPEG)
![explanation](https://dhmbxeygs57ff.cloudfront.net/uploads/ac25710341c747349404d7fb34e3e14fx800x423.PNG)

`Q10838`

## 148. Dilatation and Curettage

**PEARL:** D&C — performed with **Hegar's dilators and a Sims (or sharp) curette** — is
both diagnostic and therapeutic. Its classic diagnostic indications are **postmenopausal
bleeding** and **abnormal uterine bleeding**; it has **no role in endometriosis, PID or
cervical carcinoma**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The procedure samples the **endometrium**. It therefore answers only
questions whose answer lies within the uterine cavity. Endometriosis is by definition
**outside** the cavity (diagnosed at laparoscopy), PID is a tubal/peritoneal infection
(clinical diagnosis), and cervical cancer is diagnosed by **colposcopy and biopsy of the
cervix** — none of them can be reached or answered by curetting the endometrium.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Diagnostic:** abnormal uterine bleeding (assess hormonal pattern), **postmenopausal bleeding — to exclude endometrial carcinoma**, surveillance in women on **tamoxifen**, secondary amenorrhoea (**tuberculous endometritis** — sample in the premenstrual phase), infertility work-up.
- **Therapeutic:** evacuation of incomplete/missed abortion, MTP, removal of endometrial polyps, retained products, and molar evacuation (by **suction**, not sharp curettage).
- **Complications:** uterine perforation (the commonest — highest risk in the retroverted uterus and in pregnancy), cervical laceration, haemorrhage, infection, and **Asherman syndrome** (intrauterine adhesions from over-vigorous curettage of a pregnant uterus, presenting as secondary amenorrhoea and infertility).
- **Hysteroscopy with directed biopsy has largely replaced blind D&C**, because D&C samples less than half the cavity and misses focal lesions; **office endometrial sampling (Pipelle)** is the modern first-line test for postmenopausal bleeding, with **TVS endometrial thickness >4 mm** as the trigger.
- **Postmenopausal bleeding is endometrial carcinoma until proven otherwise** (~10% of cases).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** An instrument-image question (Hegar's dilator, Sims curette) followed
by "which other condition is this done for" — pick the one **inside the uterine cavity**.

**REMEMBER:** *A curette only knows what is inside the cavity.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://dhmbxeygs57ff.cloudfront.net/uploads/16cdc6a0524a49bf9979457a35a40885x1280x4487.JPEG)

`Q10833`

## 149. Increased Nuchal Translucency with a Normal Karyotype

**PEARL:** An increased **nuchal translucency (≥3.5 mm)** with a **NORMAL karyotype**
still demands a detailed **fetal echocardiogram in the second trimester** — the residual
risk is **congenital heart disease**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Nuchal fluid accumulates whenever lymphatic return from the head and neck
is impaired or venous pressure is raised. Aneuploidy is only one cause of that; a
**structurally abnormal heart or great vessels** raises central venous pressure and
produces exactly the same finding. So excluding aneuploidy removes one explanation, not
the finding itself — the fluid is still telling you something.

**MUST KNOW:**
- NT is measured between **11+0 and 13+6 weeks (CRL 45–84 mm)**; **≥3.5 mm** is above the 99th centile.
- Associations with a normal karyotype: **congenital heart disease (the most important), diaphragmatic hernia, skeletal dysplasias, Noonan syndrome (and other RASopathies), congenital infection, fetal anaemia, and single-gene disorders** — offer **exome/microarray** where available.
- **First-trimester combined test:** NT + **maternal free β-hCG and PAPP-A** + maternal age. In **Down syndrome: NT↑, β-hCG↑, PAPP-A↓**; in **trisomy 18 and 13: all markers low**.
- Additional first-trimester soft markers: **absent nasal bone, reversed a-wave in the ductus venosus, tricuspid regurgitation**.
- **Cystic hygroma** (septated, extending along the fetal back) carries a far worse prognosis than a simple increased NT — strongly linked to **Turner syndrome (45,XO)**.
- If NT is normal, the fetal echo is not routinely indicated; the finding is what drives the referral.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem deliberately says **"karyotype is normal"** to make you think
the work-up is finished. It isn't — **cardiac defect** is the answer.

**REMEMBER:** *Thick nuchal fold, normal chromosomes — look at the heart.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/15/picture28.jpg)
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/15/picture29.jpg)

`Q10878`

## 150. Complete Hydatidiform Mole

**PEARL:** Exaggerated nausea, a uterus large for dates and a **"snowstorm / bunch of
grapes"** ultrasound with **no fetal parts** = **complete mole**, karyotype **46,XX
(entirely PATERNAL — androgenetic diploidy)**.

**UNDERSTAND:** A complete mole arises when an **empty ovum** (no maternal nucleus) is
fertilised by a single sperm that duplicates (monospermic, 46,XX in ~90%) or by two sperm
(dispermic, 46,XX or 46,XY). Because **paternal genes drive trophoblast growth and
maternal genes drive embryonic development** (genomic imprinting), an all-paternal
conceptus produces **exuberant trophoblast and no embryo** — which explains the huge hCG,
the hyperemesis, the theca lutein cysts and the absent fetus in a single stroke.
**46,YY does not exist** — no maternal or paternal X means non-viability.

**MUST KNOW:**

| | **Complete mole** | **Partial mole** |
|---|---|---|
| Karyotype | **46,XX (or XY) — all paternal, diploid** | **69,XXY / 69,XXX — TRIPLOID**, one maternal + two paternal sets |
| Fetus | **Absent** | Present, abnormal, growth-restricted |
| Villi | **All** swollen, diffuse hydropic change | **Focal**, some normal villi, scalloped outlines |
| hCG | **Very high (>100,000)** | Mildly raised |
| Malignant potential | **15–20%** | **1–5%** |

- Features: **bleeding with grape-like vesicles**, uterus large for dates, **hyperemesis, early pre-eclampsia (<20 weeks — always think mole), hyperthyroidism** (hCG cross-reacts at the TSH receptor), **theca lutein cysts** (resolve spontaneously).
- Treatment: **suction evacuation** (never sharp curettage or medical induction — risk of embolisation and dissemination) + **anti-D if Rh-negative** + **contraception (not an IUCD) for 6–12 months** while **serial β-hCG** is monitored weekly to normal, then monthly.
- **Rising or plateauing hCG = gestational trophoblastic neoplasia** → chemotherapy (**methotrexate** for low-risk WHO score; **EMA-CO** for high-risk). GTN is one of the most curable cancers.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The question is usually the **karyotype** — complete mole **46,XX
all paternal**, partial mole **69,XXY triploid**. Don't be lured by "any of these".

**REMEMBER:** *All father, no baby — 46,XX complete. Triploid with a fetus — partial.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/15/picture40_Z2UabYR.jpg)

`Q10886`

## 151. Endometrial Carcinoma: FIGO Staging Traps

**PEARL:** **>50% myometrial invasion = Stage IB.** **Positive peritoneal washings and
lymphovascular space invasion do NOT change the FIGO stage** — washings were removed from
staging in the 2009 revision.

**UNDERSTAND:** FIGO stages by **anatomical extent of tumour spread**, not by cytological
or microvascular findings. Washings were dropped because positive cytology, on its own,
had no independent effect on survival once depth and grade were accounted for. LVSI is a
powerful **prognostic** factor that guides **adjuvant therapy**, but it is not a
compartment the tumour has physically invaded, so it stays out of the stage.

**MUST KNOW (FIGO 2009, the version examined most):**
- **IA** — no or **<50%** myometrial invasion; **IB** — **≥50%** invasion.
- **II** — **cervical STROMAL** invasion (endocervical **glandular** involvement alone is Stage I).
- **IIIA** — serosa/adnexa; **IIIB** — vagina/parametrium; **IIIC1** — pelvic nodes; **IIIC2** — para-aortic nodes.
- **IVA** — bladder/bowel mucosa; **IVB** — distant metastasis, **including inguinal nodes and intra-abdominal spread**.
- **Type I (endometrioid, ~80%)** — oestrogen-driven, from **atypical hyperplasia**, obesity/PCOS/tamoxifen/unopposed oestrogen, **PTEN, KRAS, microsatellite instability**, low grade, good prognosis. **Type II (serous, clear cell)** — oestrogen-independent, atrophic endometrium, elderly, **p53** mutation, aggressive, behaves like ovarian cancer.
- **Postmenopausal bleeding is the presenting symptom in 90%**; investigate with **TVS (endometrial thickness >4 mm) then endometrial biopsy/hysteroscopy**.
- Surgery is the mainstay: **total hysterectomy + BSO + peritoneal washings + nodal assessment (increasingly sentinel node)**; adjuvant vaginal brachytherapy or EBRT depending on grade, depth and LVSI.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem loads the stage-irrelevant findings (**LVSI, positive
washings**) to tempt you into Stage III. Anchor on the one thing that counts here —
**depth of myometrial invasion**.

**REMEMBER:** *Half the myometrium makes it IB; washings and LVSI change the treatment,
not the stage.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/15/picture45_AgNegDF.jpg)

`Q10892`

## 152. Dysgerminoma, Gonadoblastoma and the Y Chromosome

**PEARL:** A dysgerminoma in a young woman with **primary amenorrhoea** should prompt a
**karyotype** — the association is with **gonadal dysgenesis containing Y-chromosome
material (46,XY or 45,X/46,XY)**, not a plain 46,XX. **Any dysgenetic gonad with Y
material must be removed** because of **gonadoblastoma** risk.

**UNDERSTAND:** A gonad that contains Y material but fails to differentiate normally holds
germ cells sitting in a **hormonally abnormal, dysplastic stroma** — the substrate for
**gonadoblastoma**, which is itself an in-situ lesion that progresses to **dysgerminoma**
in ~50%. Removing the streak gonads removes the entire risk, which is why prophylactic
gonadectomy is standard whenever Y material is present.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Dysgerminoma** — commonest malignant ovarian germ cell tumour; marker **LDH** (± low-level hCG from syncytiotrophoblastic giant cells); histology: **sheets of clear glycogen-rich cells with lymphocytic infiltrate and fibrous septa**.
- It is the **only ovarian germ cell tumour that is commonly bilateral (10–15%)** and the **most radiosensitive** — but chemotherapy (**BEP**) is now preferred to preserve fertility.
- **Excellent prognosis at any stage**; **fertility-sparing unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy with staging preserves the uterus and the normal ovary** — but the contralateral gonad is removed if it is dysgenetic or contains gonadoblastoma.
- **Swyer syndrome (46,XY complete gonadal dysgenesis):** phenotypic female, streak gonads, **normal Müllerian structures** (no AMH was made), primary amenorrhoea, tall — needs **gonadectomy** and can carry a pregnancy with donor oocytes.
- Contrast **complete androgen insensitivity (46,XY):** female phenotype, **absent uterus, blind vagina, breasts present, no pubic hair** — gonadectomy after puberty (lower tumour risk, allows spontaneous feminisation).
- The amenorrhoea that "resolves" on the combined pill is **withdrawal bleeding**, not restored ovulation — a classic red herring in the stem.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which statement is FALSE" — the false one is the **46,XX karyotype**,
because the association being tested is with Y-containing dysgenesis.

**REMEMBER:** *Y in a streak gonad breeds gonadoblastoma — take it out.*

`Q10891`

---

## 153. Obstructed Hemivagina with Ipsilateral Renal Anomaly (OHVIRA)

**PEARL:** **Progressively worsening dysmenorrhoea despite NORMAL regular menstruation
beginning soon after menarche** = an **obstructed but incomplete outflow tract** —
**OHVIRA (Herlyn–Werner–Wunderlich) syndrome**: uterus didelphys + obstructed
hemivagina + **ipsilateral renal agenesis**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The discriminator is that **she is bleeding normally**. An imperforate
hymen, transverse vaginal septum or Müllerian agenesis all produce **primary amenorrhoea**
(or none at all) — no blood escapes. OHVIRA obstructs only **one** of two duplicated
systems, so the patent side menstruates normally while the blocked side accumulates
haematocolpos — hence cyclic, escalating, analgesia-resistant pain **with** periods.
The renal anomaly is obligatory because the **mesonephric (Wolffian) duct induces both the
ureteric bud and the fusion of the Müllerian ducts** — one embryological insult, two
organs.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Triad: **didelphys uterus, blind obstructed hemivagina, ipsilateral renal agenesis** (almost always the same side as the obstruction).
- Presents **within months of menarche** with dysmenorrhoea, a paravaginal mass, sometimes foul discharge (if a small communication exists) and later **endometriosis/haematosalpinx** from retrograde flow.
- Diagnosis: **MRI is the gold standard** (defines uterine and vaginal anatomy) plus renal ultrasound. Treatment: **vaginal septum resection/marsupialisation** — simple and curative.
- Compare the amenorrhoeic obstructions: **imperforate hymen** (cyclic pain, **bulging bluish membrane**, haematocolpos — cruciate incision); **transverse vaginal septum** (same, but no bulge at the introitus); **Müllerian agenesis (MRKH)** — absent uterus and upper vagina, **normal ovaries and secondary sexual characteristics**, **46,XX**, associated renal and skeletal anomalies, **no pain** (no endometrium).
- Any Müllerian anomaly mandates **renal imaging** — up to 30% have a urinary tract anomaly.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The clue is **normal menses plus escalating pain**. If the stem says
amenorrhoea, switch to imperforate hymen/septum/agenesis.

**REMEMBER:** *Periods present but pain worsening — one side is blocked, and that kidney
is missing.*

`Q10895`

---

## 154. Vaccination in Pregnancy

**PEARL:** **Live vaccines are contraindicated in pregnancy — but inadvertent
administration is NOT an indication for termination.** The theoretical risk has never
been shown to cause fetal harm.

**UNDERSTAND:** The contraindication is **precautionary**, based on the biological
possibility that an attenuated organism could cross the placenta — not on observed
teratogenicity. Registries of thousands of women accidentally vaccinated with rubella and
varicella near conception have found **no congenital rubella or varicella syndrome**. So
the correct response to an accidental live vaccine is **counselling and reassurance**, not
termination.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Recommended IN pregnancy:** **Tdap in every pregnancy (27–36 weeks — to give the newborn passive pertussis antibody)**, **inactivated influenza vaccine (any trimester)**, **COVID-19**, **hepatitis B** (series may be started or continued), **tetanus toxoid** (2 doses/booster per the Indian national schedule), and **rabies vaccine — never withheld, since rabies is universally fatal**.
- **Contraindicated (live):** **MMR, varicella, live influenza (LAIV), yellow fever (unless unavoidable exposure), oral typhoid, BCG**. **Delay conception by 1 month after rubella or varicella vaccination.**
- **Toxoid- and inactivated/subunit vaccines are safe** — the general rule.
- **Rubella-susceptible women should be vaccinated POSTPARTUM**, before discharge; breastfeeding is not a contraindication.
- **Anti-D immunoglobulin** is not a vaccine but follows the same instinct — give when indicated, don't withhold.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "All are true EXCEPT" — the false statement is always the one
recommending **termination** after a live vaccine.

**REMEMBER:** *Avoid live vaccines in pregnancy; never terminate for one given by mistake.*

`Q10899`

---

## 155. Zhang vs Friedman: The Modern Labour Curve

**PEARL:** In the **Zhang** curve the **active phase begins at 6 cm**; in the classic
**Friedman** curve it begins at **3–4 cm**. Modern practice (ACOG, and the WHO **Labour
Care Guide**) follows **Zhang**, not Friedman.

**UNDERSTAND:** Friedman's 1950s data came from a very different obstetric population —
heavily sedated, mostly parous women, few with epidurals or oxytocin — and produced a
sharp inflection at 3–4 cm with a fixed minimum dilatation rate of 1 cm/hour. Zhang's
contemporary cohorts showed labour is **much slower before 6 cm** and that the transition
to rapid dilatation happens late. The practical consequence is enormous: applying
Friedman's standard labels normal early labour as "arrest" and drives **unnecessary
caesarean sections**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Zhang:** no clear deceleration phase; progress from 4 to 6 cm is slow and highly variable; **rate accelerates after 6 cm**; **arrest of the active phase should not be diagnosed before 6 cm** with ruptured membranes and adequate contractions for ≥4 hours (or 6 hours with inadequate contractions).
- **Friedman:** latent phase → active phase (acceleration, **phase of maximum slope**, deceleration) → second stage; alert at **1 cm/hour** in nulliparas.
- The **WHO Labour Care Guide (2020)** replaced the classic partograph: **active phase from 5 cm**, no alert/action lines, individualised reference thresholds.
- Prolonged **second stage** definitions (ACOG): ≥3 hours in nulliparas, ≥2 hours in multiparas — **add an hour if an epidural is in place**.
- The classic partograph (**plot from 4 cm, alert line 1 cm/hr, action line 4 hours to the right**) is still what Indian PG exams ask for the **WHO modified partograph** — know both and read which is named.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Pure comparison. **Zhang = 6 cm; Friedman = 3–4 cm; Labour Care Guide
is based on Zhang, not Friedman.**

**REMEMBER:** *Friedman said four, Zhang says six — and six is why fewer caesareans.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/15/picture42_cyrT7Hh.jpg)

`Q10889`

## 156. Eclampsia and PRES

**PEARL:** Seizures, cortical blindness or confusion in severe pre-eclampsia/eclampsia,
with **bilateral symmetrical parieto-occipital white matter oedema** on MRI (bright on
**T2/FLAIR**, **no restricted diffusion**), = **posterior reversible encephalopathy
syndrome (PRES)** — and it is **reversible**.

**UNDERSTAND:** PRES is a **vasogenic** oedema from failure of cerebral autoregulation
under an abrupt pressure surge plus endothelial dysfunction. It is **posterior** because
the **vertebrobasilar circulation has sparse sympathetic innervation**, so the occipital
and parietal lobes lose autoregulatory protection first. Because the fluid is
**interstitial, not cytotoxic**, **DWI is not restricted (high ADC)** — the single feature
separating it from an infarct, and the reason it resolves when the pressure is controlled.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Presentation: headache, **visual disturbance including cortical blindness**, altered consciousness, seizures.
- Causes beyond eclampsia: **malignant hypertension, renal failure, calcineurin inhibitors (tacrolimus, ciclosporin), cytotoxic chemotherapy, sepsis, autoimmune disease, TTP**.
- **Management of eclampsia: MAGNESIUM SULPHATE** is the drug of choice for both treating and preventing seizures — superior to phenytoin and diazepam. **Pritchard/Zuspan regimens**; monitor **patellar reflexes, respiratory rate ≥12/min, urine output ≥30 mL/hr**; antidote **10% calcium gluconate 10 mL IV**.
- Control BP with **labetalol, hydralazine or nifedipine** (target ~140–150/90–100 — do not drop it precipitously, or placental perfusion fails).
- **Delivery is the definitive treatment** — but stabilise the mother first: control the seizure and the blood pressure before the caesarean.
- Postpartum eclampsia occurs up to **4 weeks** after delivery — continue magnesium for **24 hours after the last fit or after delivery**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The MRI image plus eclampsia makes PRES the answer; the distractors
are haemorrhages, which would be hyperdense on CT and asymmetrical.

**REMEMBER:** *Back of the brain, vasogenic, reversible — control the pressure and it goes.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/15/picture43_Ut01KC3.jpg)

`Q10890`

---

## 157. Anorexia Nervosa and Hypothalamic Amenorrhoea

**PEARL:** In anorexia nervosa, **neuropeptide Y is ELEVATED**, while **leptin, GnRH
pulsatility, LH, FSH and oestradiol are all LOW** — a **hypogonadotrophic hypogonadism**
of starvation.

**UNDERSTAND:** **Leptin is the fat-derived signal that permits reproduction.** With
minimal adipose tissue, leptin falls, and low leptin does two things: it **releases the
brake on neuropeptide Y** (a powerful orexigenic hypothalamic peptide that rises to drive
hunger) and it **removes the permissive signal for GnRH pulse generation**. NPY itself
further suppresses GnRH. So the body switches off reproduction and switches on
appetite-drive — the elegant logic of "not enough energy to gestate".

**MUST KNOW:**
- Endocrine profile: **LH and FSH low (prepubertal pattern), oestradiol low, leptin low, NPY high, cortisol HIGH, growth hormone HIGH with LOW IGF-1** (acquired GH resistance), **low T3 (sick euthyroid, with raised reverse T3)**.
- Complications: **osteoporosis** (the most important long-term consequence — low oestrogen plus low IGF-1), **bradycardia, hypotension, prolonged QT and arrhythmia (the commonest cause of death), hypokalaemia** (with purging), **lanugo hair**, parotid enlargement, raised amylase, **Russell's sign** (knuckle callosities in bulimia).
- **Refeeding syndrome** — hypophosphataemia, hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia, thiamine deficiency, cardiac failure → refeed slowly with phosphate and thiamine supplementation.
- Treatment: **nutritional rehabilitation and weight restoration first**; **family-based therapy is first-line in adolescents**; **fluoxetine** helps bulimia more than anorexia; the **combined pill does NOT protect the bones** — weight gain does.
- Amenorrhoea is no longer required for the DSM-5 diagnosis.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The question offers "elevated LH/GnRH/leptin" as traps — all are
**low**. Only **NPY (and cortisol, GH)** goes up.

**REMEMBER:** *No fat, no leptin, no GnRH — and NPY screams for food.*

`Q10894`

---

## 158. Stepwise Management of Atonic Postpartum Haemorrhage

**PEARL:** After **uterine massage, oxytocin and methylergometrine** have failed, the next
step is **PGF2α (carboprost) 250 µg intramyometrially or IM**, before moving to
mechanical/surgical measures (balloon tamponade → uterine/internal iliac artery ligation →
B-Lynch → hysterectomy).

**UNDERSTAND:** The escalation follows **least invasive but most effective first**.
Prostaglandins act on a different receptor system from oxytocin and ergometrine, so a
uterus unresponsive to those two may still contract to PGF2α — which makes it the last
pharmacological rung before you commit to a procedure. Skipping to surgery in a woman who
would have responded to a drug costs her the uterus unnecessarily.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Uterotonics and their cautions:** **oxytocin** (first-line; avoid rapid IV bolus — hypotension, water intoxication); **methylergometrine 0.2 mg** — **contraindicated in HYPERTENSION, pre-eclampsia and heart disease**; **carboprost (PGF2α)** — **contraindicated in ASTHMA**, causes diarrhoea, vomiting, fever, bronchospasm, may be repeated every 15 min to 8 doses; **misoprostol 800–1000 µg PR** (heat-stable, useful where refrigeration is unavailable); **tranexamic acid 1 g IV within 3 hours** (WOMAN trial — reduces death from bleeding).
- **The 4 Ts of PPH: TONE (70–80% — atony), TRAUMA (tears, rupture, inversion), TISSUE (retained products, adherent placenta), THROMBIN (coagulopathy).** Always **exclude trauma and retained tissue** — no drug will fix a cervical tear.
- Mechanical/surgical ladder: **bimanual compression → intrauterine balloon (Bakri) tamponade → uterine artery ligation (O'Leary) → B-Lynch compression suture → stepwise devascularisation/internal iliac ligation → HYSTERECTOMY** (definitive). Aortic compression and the non-pneumatic anti-shock garment buy time during transfer.
- **Risk factors for atony:** grand multiparity, overdistension (**polyhydramnios, twins, macrosomia**), prolonged or precipitate labour, chorioamnionitis, previous PPH, prolonged oxytocin use, general anaesthesia.
- **Definition: ≥500 mL after vaginal delivery, ≥1000 mL after caesarean**, or any blood loss causing haemodynamic compromise. **Active management of the third stage** (prophylactic oxytocin, controlled cord traction, uterine massage) is the key preventive measure.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem lists what has already been given — count how far up the
ladder you are. Oxytocin + ergometrine done → **prostaglandin next**, not hysterectomy.

**REMEMBER:** *Oxytocin, ergot, prostaglandin — then, and only then, reach for a
procedure.*

`Q10941`

---

## 159. Asherman Syndrome: When the Risk Is Highest

**PEARL:** The risk of **Asherman syndrome (intrauterine adhesions)** is **highest when D&C
is performed on the POSTPARTUM uterus** — for postpartum haemorrhage or retained products
— and lowest when it is done on a **non-pregnant** uterus (e.g. for abnormal uterine
bleeding).

**UNDERSTAND:** Adhesions form when the **basal layer of the endometrium is destroyed** so
that regeneration cannot occur and the raw myometrial surfaces oppose and fuse. Two things
make the postpartum uterus uniquely vulnerable: it is **soft, thin-walled and oestrogen-
deficient (lactational hypo-oestrogenism prevents re-epithelialisation)**, and the curette
therefore removes the basalis far too easily. The same operation on a firm, oestrogenised
non-pregnant uterus rarely reaches that depth.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Relative risk order: **postpartum curettage (highest) > curettage for missed/incomplete abortion > MTP > D&C for AUB (lowest)**. Risk rises with **repeated** curettage and with concurrent **infection (genital tuberculosis is a major cause of severe Asherman in India)**.
- Presentation: **secondary amenorrhoea or hypomenorrhoea with cyclical pain, infertility, recurrent pregnancy loss, and abnormal placentation (accreta) in later pregnancies**, all after a uterine procedure.
- **Diagnosis: HYSTEROSCOPY is the gold standard** (direct visualisation and simultaneous treatment); **hysterosalpingography** shows irregular filling defects; saline infusion sonography is an alternative. Withdrawal bleeding fails to occur after **oestrogen + progesterone challenge** — which localises the defect to the **outflow/endometrium**, not the ovary or pituitary.
- **Treatment: hysteroscopic adhesiolysis + prevention of re-adhesion** (intrauterine balloon/IUCD/hyaluronic acid gel) + **high-dose oestrogen** to regenerate endometrium; pregnancy rates depend on the initial severity.
- **Prevention:** use **suction rather than sharp curettage**, ultrasound guidance, avoid over-vigorous curettage postpartum, treat infection promptly, and prefer **medical management of miscarriage** where possible.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The question is a ranking, not a definition. **Postpartum = softest
uterus = highest risk.**

**REMEMBER:** *A soft, unoestrogenised uterus scars — curette the puerperal uterus and you
lose the basalis.*

`Q10948`

---

## 160. Diabetes in Pregnancy: Why Insulin

**PEARL:** **Insulin does NOT cross the placenta**; **sulfonylureas such as tolbutamide DO**
— risking **fetal hyperinsulinaemia and neonatal hypoglycaemia**. Insulin requirements
**rise steeply through pregnancy** and cannot be met by oral agents. **Sulfonylureas do NOT
cause pregnancy-induced hypertension.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The placenta produces **human placental lactogen, progesterone, cortisol and
prolactin — all insulin antagonists** — so insulin resistance rises progressively, roughly
doubling to trebling requirements by the third trimester. That physiological antagonism is
also why gestational diabetes appears in the **second half** of pregnancy and why
requirements **crash immediately after delivery of the placenta** (halve the dose at once).

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Insulin is the gold standard** in pregnancy: it is a **large polypeptide that does not cross the placenta**, and short-acting analogues (**lispro, aspart**) and **NPH/detemir** are established as safe.
- **METFORMIN crosses the placenta but is widely used and considered safe** (used in GDM and PCOS); **glyburide/glibenclamide crosses in small amounts**; older sulfonylureas (tolbutamide, chlorpropamide) cross freely and are avoided.
- **Screening: 75 g OGTT at 24–28 weeks** (earlier if high-risk). **DIPSI (India): 75 g non-fasting, 2-hour value ≥140 mg/dL diagnoses GDM.** IADPSG/WHO thresholds: fasting ≥92, 1 h ≥180, 2 h ≥153 mg/dL.
- **Targets: fasting <95 mg/dL, 1-hour postprandial <140, 2-hour <120; HbA1c <6.0–6.5%.**
- **Pre-existing diabetes:** **preconception HbA1c <6.5%, high-dose folic acid 5 mg**, stop ACE inhibitors/ARBs and statins, screen retina and renal function. **Poor first-trimester control causes congenital malformations — caudal regression syndrome is the most characteristic, cardiac and neural tube defects the commonest.**
- Fetal risks later: **macrosomia and shoulder dystocia, polyhydramnios, stillbirth, neonatal hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia, polycythaemia, hyperbilirubinaemia and respiratory distress** (insulin antagonises surfactant production).
- Postpartum: **insulin requirements fall abruptly**; re-screen with an OGTT at **6–12 weeks**; lifetime risk of type 2 diabetes ~50%.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which statement is NOT true?" — the false one attaches a wrong adverse
effect (**PIH**) to the oral agent; the placental-transfer facts are all true.

**REMEMBER:** *Insulin is too big to cross; the sulfonylurea slips through and feeds the
fetal pancreas.*

`Q10949`

---

## 161. DMPA (Depot Medroxyprogesterone Acetate)

**PEARL:** DMPA **DOES protect against endometrial carcinoma** — the statement that it does
not is false. It is **150 mg IM every 3 months**, safe in **epilepsy** (it may even raise
the seizure threshold), useful for **menorrhagia**, and has a typical-use failure rate of
about **3–4%** (perfect use <1%).

**UNDERSTAND:** Every benefit follows from **continuous, unopposed progestogen action**:
the endometrium is rendered **thin and atrophic**, which simultaneously **reduces bleeding**
and **removes the oestrogen-driven proliferation that leads to hyperplasia and carcinoma**.
The same atrophy explains the commonest side effect — **irregular spotting progressing to
amenorrhoea** — which is a therapeutic effect misread as a problem, and the main reason
women discontinue it.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Mechanism:** suppression of the **LH surge → anovulation** (primary), plus thick cervical mucus and endometrial atrophy.
- **Advantages:** **no oestrogen** — so it is safe in **smokers over 35, hypertension, migraine with aura, and a history of VTE**; usable while **breastfeeding**; reduces **dysmenorrhoea, menorrhagia, endometriosis pain, and sickle cell crises**; protects against **endometrial cancer and PID**.
- **Disadvantages:** **delayed return of fertility (median 9–10 months after the last injection)**, weight gain, mood change, **REVERSIBLE LOSS OF BONE MINERAL DENSITY** (a black-box warning — of most concern in adolescents and beyond 2 years of use), irregular bleeding then amenorrhoea.
- **Not affected by enzyme-inducing antiepileptics** (unlike the combined pill and implants) — a key reason it is chosen in epilepsy.
- Related long-acting methods: **implants (etonogestrel, levonorgestrel)**, **LNG-IUS (Mirena)** — the LNG-IUS is the **first-line medical treatment for heavy menstrual bleeding** and protects the endometrium during oestrogen HRT and tamoxifen therapy.
- **Combined oral contraceptives** also protect against **endometrial and ovarian cancer** (a durable benefit), while slightly increasing breast and cervical cancer risk and VTE.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which statement is NOT true?" — the false option denies a genuine
protective effect. Progestogens **protect** the endometrium; unopposed **oestrogen** is what
causes cancer.

**REMEMBER:** *Progestogen thins the endometrium — less bleeding, less cancer, less
fertility for a while.*

`Q10950`

---

## 162. Placenta Accreta Spectrum: Counselling Before Repeat Caesarean

**PEARL:** A multiparous woman with **two previous caesareans** must be consented for the
**placenta accreta spectrum**: **massive haemorrhage and transfusion, BLADDER and ureteric
injury, and caesarean HYSTERECTOMY**. **Bowel injury is not a typical consequence** — the
placenta invades **forward into the bladder**, not backward.

**UNDERSTAND:** Accreta occurs where the **decidua basalis and Nitabuch's layer are absent
over a scar**, so villi implant directly into myometrium. The uterine scar and a
**praevia** lie in the **lower anterior segment, immediately behind the bladder**, so
invasion (**percreta**) tracks anteriorly. That anatomy dictates the entire risk profile —
urological, not intestinal.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Grades: accreta** (villi attached to myometrium), **increta** (invade myometrium), **percreta** (through serosa, often into **bladder**).
- **Risk = previous caesarean + placenta praevia**, and it multiplies: with praevia, the risk of accreta is ~3% after one caesarean, ~11% after two, **>60% after four or more**. Other risks: previous myomectomy, curettage, **Asherman syndrome**, submucous fibroids, advanced maternal age.
- **Diagnosis antenatally: greyscale ultrasound with colour Doppler** — loss of the retroplacental clear zone, **placental lacunae ("moth-eaten"/Swiss-cheese)**, bladder-wall interruption, increased subplacental vascularity; **MRI** for posterior placentas or to assess parametrial/bladder invasion.
- **Management: planned caesarean hysterectomy at 34–36 weeks in a tertiary centre** with a multidisciplinary team, cross-matched blood, cell salvage, urology on standby, ± ureteric stents or interventional radiology balloons. **Do NOT attempt to remove the placenta** — leave it in situ and proceed to hysterectomy.
- Antenatal corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity given the planned preterm delivery.
- Consent must include: **hysterectomy, massive transfusion, bladder/ureteric injury, ICU admission, re-laparotomy, and loss of fertility.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which is NOT required in the consent?" — the answer is the injury that
is anatomically implausible (**bowel**). Everything urological or haemorrhagic is required.

**REMEMBER:** *The scar and the placenta sit in front of the bladder — that's what gets
injured.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://dhmbxeygs57ff.cloudfront.net/uploads/84268612f965433bacb61fb002a017edx600x1200.PNG)

`Q10967`

## 163. Blood Volume Expansion in Pregnancy

**PEARL:** The **50% rise in plasma volume** is driven by **OESTROGEN (directly, and by
activating the renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system) and ALDOSTERONE**, both acting through
**increased renal sodium and water retention**. **ADH levels are UNCHANGED** — only the
osmotic threshold is reset downward.

**UNDERSTAND:** Pregnancy resets the body's set-point rather than switching on a new
hormone. **Progesterone and oestrogen cause systemic vasodilatation**, which the kidney reads
as **underfilling**; RAAS is activated in response, and aldosterone retains sodium and
water until the enlarged vascular space is refilled. Simultaneously the **osmostat is reset
about 10 mOsm/kg lower**, so a serum sodium of ~135 mmol/L is normal and ADH secretion
continues appropriately at that lower osmolality — hence **normal, not raised, ADH**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Plasma volume rises ~45–50%; red cell mass rises only ~20–30%** → **physiological (dilutional) anaemia of pregnancy**, maximal at **32 weeks**. WHO defines anaemia in pregnancy as **Hb <11 g/dL**.
- Benefits of hypervolaemia: **lower viscosity improves uteroplacental perfusion**, buffers against **aortocaval compression**, and provides a reserve for **delivery blood loss (~500 mL vaginal, ~1000 mL caesarean)**.
- Related renal changes: **GFR and renal plasma flow rise ~50%** → **serum creatinine and urea FALL** (a "normal" creatinine may be abnormal in pregnancy), **physiological glycosuria**, and **dilatation of the collecting systems, right more than left**.
- Also rises: **fibrinogen and factors VII, VIII, IX, X (a hypercoagulable state — VTE is a leading cause of maternal death)**, ESR, alkaline phosphatase (placental), thyroid-binding globulin and total T4 (free T4 normal).
- Falls: **colloid oncotic pressure, haematocrit, serum sodium and osmolality, urea, creatinine, bicarbonate (compensating the progesterone-driven respiratory alkalosis)**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Both A and B" is correct because the hormones and the renal mechanism
are the same phenomenon described at two levels. Reject any option naming **ADH**.

**REMEMBER:** *Oestrogen and aldosterone tell the kidney to hold salt and water — ADH just
resets its threshold.*

`Q10955`

---

## 164. DMPA: The Grace Period

**PEARL:** DMPA is given **every 3 months (13 weeks)**, with a **GRACE PERIOD of up to 4
WEEKS late** — a woman presenting **14 days late may simply receive her injection, with no
back-up contraception and no pregnancy test**. Beyond 4 weeks, do a **urine pregnancy test
first** and advise 7 days of back-up.

**UNDERSTAND:** The grace period exists because DMPA's **ovulation-suppressing levels
persist well beyond the nominal 12–13 weeks** — its effect is limited by the slow release
from the intramuscular depot, not by a sharp cut-off. Only when that pharmacological tail
has genuinely run out (beyond ~4 months from the last dose) does ovulation become possible,
and only then does pregnancy need excluding.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Dose: 150 mg IM (or 104 mg subcutaneous) every 13 weeks.** If started **within the first 7 days of the cycle**, no back-up is needed; if started later, use back-up for **7 days**.
- **Mechanism:** suppression of the **LH surge → anovulation**, plus thickened cervical mucus and an atrophic endometrium.
- **Side effects:** irregular bleeding progressing to **amenorrhoea (a therapeutic effect, and the commonest reason for discontinuation — counsel in advance)**, weight gain, mood change, **reversible loss of bone mineral density**, and **delayed return of fertility (median 9–10 months)** — so it is a poor choice for a woman planning pregnancy soon.
- **Benefits:** oestrogen-free (safe in **smokers >35, hypertension, migraine with aura, past VTE, and breastfeeding**), **protects against endometrial carcinoma and PID**, reduces **menorrhagia, dysmenorrhoea, endometriosis pain and sickle cell crises**, and is **unaffected by enzyme-inducing antiepileptics**.
- **Postpartum:** can be given **immediately after delivery, including in breastfeeding women**.
- Compare **Antara programme (India)** — the injectable MPA offered free under the national family planning programme.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem gives the number of days late. **Within 4 weeks → just inject.
Beyond 4 weeks → pregnancy test first.**

**REMEMBER:** *Four weeks of grace — inject and reassure.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://dhmbxeygs57ff.cloudfront.net/uploads/86bd1bc1e387428a871ff147a72ba92dx398x265.PNG)

## 165. Borderline Ovarian Tumours

**PEARL:** **Borderline (low malignant potential) ovarian tumours** show **atypical
epithelial proliferation WITHOUT stromal invasion**. Risk factors are those of **unopposed
oestrogen exposure — LOW PARITY, infertility, obesity and smoking**. **Pregnancy, OCP use
and PCOS-related anovulation are NOT risk factors** (parity and the pill are protective).

**UNDERSTAND:** The "incessant ovulation" hypothesis explains it: every ovulation causes
**epithelial disruption and repair of the ovarian surface**, and each repair cycle is an
opportunity for a mutation. Anything that **suppresses ovulation — pregnancy, lactation,
the combined pill — reduces the risk**, and anything that **maximises lifetime ovulations —
low parity, early menarche, late menopause, nulliparity — increases it.** The same logic
governs invasive epithelial ovarian cancer.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Diagnosis is HISTOLOGICAL** — the absence of stromal invasion is what defines it; frozen section is unreliable, so final pathology decides.
- **Types: serous (commonest, may be bilateral, may show non-invasive "implants") and mucinous.** **Mucinous borderline tumours require APPENDICECTOMY** at surgery, because an appendiceal mucinous neoplasm may be the true primary and to prevent/assess **pseudomyxoma peritonei**.
- **Treatment is SURGICAL ONLY** — **fertility-sparing unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy with staging** in young women; **chemotherapy and radiotherapy have NO role**. Prognosis is excellent (5-year survival >95%) but **late recurrences occur**, so long-term follow-up is required.
- Contrast **epithelial ovarian carcinoma:** protective factors are **multiparity, breastfeeding, OCP use, tubal ligation and salpingectomy**; risk factors are **nulliparity, early menarche/late menopause, endometriosis (clear cell and endometrioid), and BRCA1/BRCA2 and Lynch syndrome**. **CA-125** for monitoring; most present late (**stage III**) with ascites and omental caking.
- **BRCA1 carriers** are advised **risk-reducing bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy at 35–40** after childbearing.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which increases the risk?" — pick the factor that means **more lifetime
ovulations**. OCPs and high parity are always the protective distractors.

**REMEMBER:** *Every ovulation is a wound — the fewer pregnancies, the more wounds.*

`Q10997`

---

## 166. Abruptio Placentae with Fetal Death

**PEARL:** Painful antepartum haemorrhage with a **tense, tender uterus larger than dates,
absent fetal heart sounds, and pre-eclampsia** = **abruptio placentae with fetal demise**.
If the mother is stable and the cervix is favourable/already dilating, the management is
**antihypertensives + ARTIFICIAL RUPTURE OF MEMBRANES and VAGINAL delivery** — not
caesarean.

**UNDERSTAND:** Once the fetus is dead, the only remaining goal is **maternal safety**, and
a caesarean adds surgical bleeding to a patient who is already **coagulopathic** (abruption
consumes fibrinogen and causes DIC in up to 30% of severe cases). **Amniotomy is
therapeutic, not merely expedient**: releasing liquor **reduces intrauterine pressure,
decreases further thromboplastin entry into the maternal circulation, and accelerates
labour** — an abrupted uterus is typically already contracting strongly. Tocolytics are
therefore exactly wrong.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Grades (Page): 0 — retrospective diagnosis on placental examination; 1 — mild, no fetal distress; 2 — fetus alive but distressed; 3 — FETAL DEATH (3a without, 3b WITH coagulopathy).**
- **Risk factors: HYPERTENSION/pre-eclampsia (the most important), previous abruption (the strongest single predictor), trauma, smoking, cocaine, polyhydramnios with sudden decompression, PPROM, thrombophilia, advanced maternal age and multiparity.**
- **Concealed haemorrhage** (blood trapped behind the placenta) means the **visible bleeding grossly underestimates the loss** — shock out of proportion to the external bleed is the hallmark; a **COUVELAIRE uterus** (blood extravasated into the myometrium, bluish and boggy) may be found at caesarean and is **not itself an indication for hysterectomy**.
- **Diagnosis is CLINICAL** — **ultrasound is insensitive** (a normal scan does not exclude abruption).
- **Caesarean is indicated when the FETUS IS ALIVE and viable with distress, or when the mother is unstable/cannot deliver quickly.**
- Complications: **hypovolaemic shock, DIC, acute kidney injury (acute tubular or cortical necrosis), postpartum haemorrhage from an atonic Couvelaire uterus, Sheehan syndrome, and fetal death.** Check **fibrinogen, platelets, PT/aPTT and cross-match**; give **anti-D** if Rh-negative (Kleihauer test).
- Contrast **placenta praevia — PAINLESS, causeless, recurrent bleeding with a SOFT, NON-TENDER uterus and a high presenting part; NEVER perform a vaginal examination** until praevia is excluded by ultrasound.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem gives the fetal status. **Dead fetus + stable mother + labour
= vaginal delivery with amniotomy.** Every caesarean option is a trap.

**REMEMBER:** *Once the baby is lost, protect the mother — rupture the membranes and deliver
from below.*

`Q11034`

---

## 167. Virilising and Feminising Ovarian Tumours

**PEARL:** The **virilising** ovarian tumour is the **ARRHENOBLASTOMA (Sertoli–Leydig cell
tumour)**. The **feminising (oestrogenic)** tumours are the **granulosa cell tumour and the
THECOMA**. **Brenner tumour and carcinoid are neither.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Sex cord–stromal tumours secrete the hormone of **the cell they imitate**.
A tumour of **Sertoli and Leydig cells** — the male gonadal cell types — makes **androgens**,
so the woman develops **defeminisation then virilisation**; a tumour of **granulosa and
theca cells** — the female follicular cells — makes **oestrogen**, so the patient develops
**endometrial stimulation**. Recognising the cell of origin gives you the syndrome without
memorising it.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Sertoli–Leydig (arrhenoblastoma):** reproductive-age women; **oligomenorrhoea → amenorrhoea, hirsutism, acne, clitoromegaly, deepening of the voice, male-pattern balding**; **REINKE CRYSTALS** in the Leydig component; raised **testosterone**; usually unilateral, low-grade, and cured by unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy. Associated with **DICER1** mutations.
- **Granulosa cell tumour:** **oestrogen** → **precocious puberty in girls, menorrhagia in adults, POSTMENOPAUSAL BLEEDING with ENDOMETRIAL HYPERPLASIA or CARCINOMA in ~5–25%**; **CALL–EXNER bodies** and **"coffee-bean" grooved nuclei**; marker **INHIBIN** (and AMH); **FOXL2 mutation** in the adult type; characteristically **recurs late (10+ years)**.
- **Thecoma** — oestrogenic, benign, in postmenopausal women; **fibroma** is its hormonally inert relative (→ **Meigs syndrome**).
- **Brenner tumour** — benign transitional (urothelial-like) epithelium with **"COFFEE-BEAN" nuclei and WALTHARD cell rests**, solid and fibrous, usually an incidental finding.
- **Struma ovarii** — a monodermal teratoma of thyroid tissue → **hyperthyroidism**; **ovarian carcinoid** → **flushing and diarrhoea without hepatic metastases** (its venous drainage bypasses the portal system).
- **Investigate any virilised woman for: PCOS (mild), congenital adrenal hyperplasia, Cushing syndrome, and an androgen-secreting ovarian or ADRENAL tumour — a testosterone >200 ng/dL or DHEAS very high suggests a tumour.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Match hormone to cell. **"Virilising ovarian tumour" is
arrhenoblastoma**; if the stem says postmenopausal bleeding with a solid ovarian mass, think
**granulosa cell tumour**.

**REMEMBER:** *Sertoli–Leydig makes a man of her; granulosa and theca make her bleed.*

`Q11010`

---

## 168. Imminent Eclampsia

**PEARL:** The premonitory symptoms of **imminent eclampsia** in a pre-eclamptic woman are
**severe persistent HEADACHE, VISUAL DISTURBANCE (blurring, scotomata, flashes),
EPIGASTRIC or right upper quadrant PAIN, and VOMITING**, often with **hyperreflexia and
clonus**. **Persistent pedal oedema is NOT a warning sign** — it is common and non-specific
in normal pregnancy.

**UNDERSTAND:** Each warning symptom localises to an **end organ already failing from
endothelial dysfunction and vasospasm**. Headache and visual change signal **cerebral and
retinal vasospasm with impending PRES/seizure**; epigastric pain signals **hepatic
subcapsular oedema or haemorrhage stretching Glisson's capsule** (the harbinger of
**HELLP** and of hepatic rupture). Oedema, by contrast, reflects only sodium and water
retention — which is why it was removed from the diagnostic criteria for pre-eclampsia
altogether.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Pre-eclampsia = new hypertension ≥140/90 after 20 weeks WITH proteinuria (≥300 mg/24 h, or protein:creatinine ratio ≥0.3, or dipstick 2+) OR any end-organ dysfunction (thrombocytopenia, renal impairment, raised transaminases, pulmonary oedema, neurological or visual symptoms) — proteinuria is no longer mandatory.**
- **Severe features: BP ≥160/110, platelets <100,000, creatinine >1.1 mg/dL (or doubled), transaminases twice normal, pulmonary oedema, new cerebral or visual symptoms, persistent epigastric pain.**
- **MAGNESIUM SULPHATE is the drug of choice for preventing and treating eclamptic seizures** (Pritchard/Zuspan regimens) — monitor **patellar reflexes, respiratory rate ≥12/min and urine output ≥30 mL/hr**; antidote **10% calcium gluconate**. Continue for **24 hours after delivery or the last fit**.
- **Antihypertensives for BP ≥160/110: labetalol, hydralazine or oral nifedipine.** Do not over-correct — placental perfusion is pressure-dependent.
- **Delivery is the only cure.** Timing: **≥37 weeks for pre-eclampsia without severe features; ≥34 weeks (after steroids) with severe features; immediate delivery for eclampsia, HELLP, abruption, non-reassuring fetal status or uncontrollable hypertension** — after stabilising the mother.
- **HELLP syndrome: Haemolysis (schistocytes, raised LDH and bilirubin), Elevated Liver enzymes, Low Platelets** — may occur with only mildly raised blood pressure.
- **Prevention: LOW-DOSE ASPIRIN (75–150 mg) started at 12–16 weeks** in high-risk women, plus calcium supplementation in low-intake populations.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Multi-select of symptoms. Include **headache, visual disturbance,
epigastric pain (and vomiting)**; exclude **oedema**.

**REMEMBER:** *Head, eyes and epigastrium — the brain, retina and liver warning you before
the fit.*

`Q11227`

---

## 169. Reading the Partogram: When Instruments Are Not an Option

**PEARL:** With the cervix **fully dilated but the head still 3/5 palpable per abdomen**,
**moulding 2+**, meconium-stained liquor and fetal heart decelerations despite adequate
oxytocin-augmented contractions, the diagnosis is **CEPHALOPELVIC DISPROPORTION with fetal
distress → EMERGENCY CAESAREAN SECTION.** **Neither forceps nor vacuum can be applied.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Instrumental delivery has an absolute anatomical prerequisite: the head must
be **ENGAGED, with 0/5 or at most 1/5 palpable abdominally and the station at or below the
ischial spines**. A head that is still **3/5 palpable has not engaged**, so the widest
diameter has not passed the pelvic brim — traction would simply pull the head against bone,
risking maternal and fetal trauma. Full dilatation alone is never sufficient; **descent is
what licenses an instrument.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Prerequisites for instrumental delivery ("FORCEPS"): Fully dilated cervix, Occiput/position known, Ruptured membranes, Cephalic presentation and Contractions present, Engaged head with an EMPTY BLADDER, Pain relief adequate, Sufficient indication and a Skilled operator with a plan B.**
- **Moulding grading: 0 — bones separate; 1+ — bones touching; 2+ — overlapped but REDUCIBLE; 3+ — overlapped and NOT reducible (severe CPD).** **Caput** is scalp oedema and is graded separately; increasing caput with poor descent is another sign of obstruction.
- **Partogram signs of obstructed labour: cervical dilatation crossing the ACTION LINE, poor or arrested descent, increasing moulding and caput, a Bandl's ring, and maternal exhaustion with fetal distress.**
- **Liquor codes: I intact, C clear, M meconium, B blood, A absent.** **Thick meconium with fetal heart decelerations means fetal compromise.**
- **Vacuum vs forceps: vacuum requires maternal effort and causes CEPHALHAEMATOMA and subgaleal haemorrhage; forceps have a higher rate of maternal perineal trauma (third/fourth-degree tears) and facial nerve injury.** Vacuum is contraindicated **before 34 weeks** and in face presentation.
- **Neglected obstructed labour → uterine rupture, vesicovaginal fistula, sepsis and fetal death** — the classic causes of maternal morbidity in low-resource settings.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Look for the **fifths palpable** or the **station**, not the dilatation.
A head palpable abdominally excludes every instrumental option.

**REMEMBER:** *Fully dilated is not enough — if the head is still palpable above the brim,
it is a caesarean.*


**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/07/04/whatsapp-image-2023-06-12-at-1901210142.jpg)

`Q11282`

## 170. The Biophysical Profile

**PEARL:** The **BPP (Manning score)** has **five components — NON-STRESS TEST, amniotic
fluid volume, fetal BREATHING movements, gross body MOVEMENTS and fetal TONE** — each scored
0 or 2 over **30 minutes**. The score has a **DIRECT, CONSISTENT relationship with fetal
pH** at all gestational ages: **a score <4 predicts a pH <7.20, and a score of 0 predicts
100% acidaemia.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The components are lost in the **reverse order of their development in
utero** — the "**gradual hypoxia concept**". **Tone appears earliest (7–8 weeks) and is lost
LAST; fetal breathing appears latest (20–21 weeks) and is lost FIRST**, followed by
movement, then tone. The reason is that the **most recently developed centres are the most
oxygen-sensitive**. Amniotic fluid volume is different — it reflects **chronic** placental
insufficiency (reduced renal perfusion → oligohydramnios), not acute hypoxia — which is why
**oligohydramnios is the most ominous single finding** even with an otherwise reassuring
score.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Score interpretation: 8–10 normal (repeat routinely); 6 equivocal — repeat in 24 hours or deliver if term; 4 suspected asphyxia — deliver if ≥32 weeks; 0–2 — DELIVER immediately.** **An abnormal NST or oligohydramnios overrides an otherwise reassuring score.**
- **MODIFIED BPP = NST + AMNIOTIC FLUID INDEX** — a quicker screening combination (an acute marker plus a chronic marker) with equivalent predictive value.
- **AFI <5 cm (or deepest vertical pocket <2 cm) = oligohydramnios.** Causes: **ruptured membranes, placental insufficiency/IUGR, post-term pregnancy, renal agenesis (POTTER sequence), posterior urethral valves, and NSAIDs/ACE inhibitors.**
- **NST: REACTIVE = ≥2 accelerations of ≥15 bpm lasting ≥15 seconds in 20 minutes** (in a fetus ≥32 weeks). A non-reactive trace is most often due to a **fetal sleep cycle** — extend the observation or use vibroacoustic stimulation before assuming compromise.
- **Umbilical artery Doppler is the single most useful test in growth-restricted fetuses: absent or REVERSED end-diastolic flow indicates severe placental insufficiency and mandates delivery.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "All are true EXCEPT" — the false statement is that the score is
**inconsistent** with fetal pH. It is consistent and linear; that is the whole point of the
test.

**REMEMBER:** *Breathing goes first, tone goes last — and a dry uterus is the worst sign of
all.*


**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/08/06/screenshot-2024-08-06-171706.png)

`Q11283`
